3-Hour Exam Time Budget (20 Questions / 100 Marks Strategy)

Part A (10 Short Answers × 3 Marks = 30M): Allocate 5 mins per answer (50 mins total) — target ½ to 1 page highlighting key formula and core definition.
Part B (10 Essay Answers × 7 Marks = 70M): Allocate 12 mins per answer (120 mins total) — target 1.5 to 2 pages with schematic circuit/block diagrams and step-by-step derivations.
Strategy & Review Buffer (10 mins): 5 mins at start for question choice planning + 5 mins at end for question numbering and equation verification.

Course Code: EET308 • 5 Modules • 26 Questions

Comprehensive Course Work

Capstone synthesis of core electrical & electronics disciplines: Circuit analysis, electromagnetic field theory, electrical machines (transformers, DC & induction/synchronous motors), power systems, control engineering, power electronics, analog/digital electronics, and microprocessor/microcontroller systems.

Module 1

Electrical Circuits & Electromagnetic Fields

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

Question 1 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Analytical ID: COMP-Q1

Q1. State and prove Thévenin's, Norton's, and the Maximum Power Transfer theorems for DC and AC circuits. Derive the complex load condition for maximum active power transfer. Q1. State and prove Thévenin's, Norton's, and the Maximum Power Transfer theorems for DC and AC circuits. Derive the complex load condition for maximum active power transfer. Q1. State and prove Thévenin's, Norton's, and the Maximum Power Transfer theorems for DC and AC circuits. Derive the complex load condition for maximum active power transfer.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (EET308) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 11(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$R_L = R_{th}, \quad P_{max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}, \quad \mathbf{Z}_L = \mathbf{Z}_{th}^* = R_{th} - jX_{th}, \quad \eta = 50\%$$

Maximum Power Transfer: In DC circuits, maximum power is delivered when $R_L = R_{th}$ with $\eta = 50\%$. In AC circuits with complex load impedance, maximum active power occurs under conjugate match $\mathbf{Z}_L = \mathbf{Z}_{th}^*$, eliminating net loop reactance.

⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$R_L = R_{th}, \quad P_{max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}, \quad \mathbf{Z}_L = \mathbf{Z}_{th}^* = R_{th} - jX_{th}, \quad \eta = 50\%$$

Maximum Power Transfer: In DC circuits, maximum power is delivered when $R_L = R_{th}$ with $\eta = 50\%$. In AC circuits with complex load impedance, maximum active power occurs under conjugate match $\mathbf{Z}_L = \mathbf{Z}_{th}^*$, eliminating net loop reactance.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Network theorems form the fundamental analytical toolkit for simplifying complex, multiloop electrical networks into manageable equivalent representations. In linear, bilateral lumped-parameter circuits, Thévenin's, Norton's, Superposition, and the Maximum Power Transfer theorems provide rigorous methodologies for evaluating branch currents, node voltages, and power dissipation.

1. Thévenin's and Norton's Theorems: - Thévenin's Theorem states that any linear, bilateral, active two-terminal network containing independent or dependent sources and impedances can be replaced at its terminal pair $(A-B)$ by an equivalent circuit consisting of a single ideal voltage source $\mathbf{V}_{th}$ in series with an equivalent impedance $\mathbf{Z}_{th}$. - The open-circuit voltage $\mathbf{V}_{th}$ is the potential difference appearing across terminals $A-B$ when the load impedance $\mathbf{Z}_L$ is disconnected ($\mathbf{V}_{th} = \mathbf{V}_{oc}$). - The Thévenin impedance $\mathbf{Z}_{th}$ is the impedance looking back into the de-energized network across terminals $A-B$ with all independent voltage sources replaced by short circuits ($V=0$) and all independent current sources replaced by open circuits ($I=0$), while retaining all dependent sources. - Norton's Theorem is the dual of Thévenin's theorem. It replaces the network by an ideal short-circuit current source $\mathbf{I}_N$ connected in parallel with an internal Norton admittance $\mathbf{Y}_N$ (or impedance $\mathbf{Z}_N$). - The Norton current is the current flowing through a direct short circuit placed across terminals $A-B$: $$\mathbf{I}_N = \mathbf{I}_{sc} = \frac{\mathbf{V}_{th}}{\mathbf{Z}_{th}}$$ - The Norton impedance equals the Thévenin impedance: $\mathbf{Z}_N = \mathbf{Z}_{th}$.

2. Superposition Theorem: The Superposition Theorem states that in any linear, bilateral electrical network energized by multiple independent sources, the resultant current or voltage in any branch is the algebraic (or phasor) sum of the currents or voltages produced by each independent source acting alone, with all other independent sources replaced by their respective internal impedances (independent voltage sources short-circuited and independent current sources open-circuited). Dependent sources are left unaltered throughout the decomposition. $$\mathbf{I}_k = \sum_{m=1}^{N_v} \mathbf{I}_{k, V_m} + \sum_{n=1}^{N_i} \mathbf{I}_{k, I_n}$$ Note that superposition applies strictly to linear responses (current and voltage) and cannot be applied directly to non-linear power quantities ($P \neq \sum P_i$ because $P \propto I^2$).

3. Maximum Power Transfer Theorem: The Maximum Power Transfer Theorem defines the necessary and sufficient conditions under which an active source network delivers maximum real active power $P_L$ to an adjustable load.

Case A: DC Source and Resistive Load ($R_L$ variable): Consider a DC network represented by its Thévenin equivalent $(V_{th}, R_{th})$ feeding a variable load resistor $R_L$. The loop current is: $$I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L}$$ The real power absorbed by the load is: $$P_L = I_L^2 R_L = \left( \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} \right)^2 R_L = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2}$$ To determine the condition for maximum load power, we take the first derivative of $P_L$ with respect to $R_L$ and set it to zero: $$\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = V_{th}^2 \cdot \frac{(R_{th} + R_L)^2 \cdot 1 - R_L \cdot 2(R_{th} + R_L)}{(R_{th} + R_L)^4} = 0$$ $$(R_{th} + R_L) - 2 R_L = 0 \implies R_{th} - R_L = 0 \implies R_L = R_{th}$$ Checking the second derivative confirms a true maximum: $$\left. \frac{d^2 P_L}{dR_L^2} \right|_{R_L = R_{th}} = -\frac{V_{th}^2}{8 R_{th}^3} < 0$$ Thus, maximum power transfer in a DC circuit occurs when the load resistance equals the internal Thévenin resistance ($R_L = R_{th}$). Under this matched condition, the maximum power delivered is: $$P_{L,\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_{th}}{(R_{th} + R_{th})^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_{th}}{4 R_{th}^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}$$ The total power generated by the source is: $$P_{source} = V_{th} I_L = V_{th} \left( \frac{V_{th}}{2 R_{th}} \right) = \frac{V_{th}^2}{2 R_{th}}$$ The transmission efficiency at maximum power transfer is: $$\eta = \frac{P_{L,\max}}{P_{source}} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{th}^2 / (4 R_{th})}{V_{th}^2 / (2 R_{th})} \times 100\% = 50\%$$ Half the generated power is unavoidably dissipated as heat inside the internal source resistance $R_{th}$. Consequently, maximum power transfer is critical in communications and RF electronics (where signal strength is in microwatts/milliwatts and reflection must be eliminated), whereas power systems prioritize transmission efficiency ($\eta > 95\%$) by operating with source internal impedances far smaller than the load impedance ($R_{th} \ll R_L$).

Case B: AC Source and Complex Load Impedance ($\mathbf{Z}_L = R_L + jX_L$ with both $R_L$ and $X_L$ independently variable): Let the source network be represented by its AC Thévenin equivalent $\mathbf{V}_{th} = V_{th} \angle 0^\circ$ and $\mathbf{Z}_{th} = R_{th} + jX_{th}$. The load impedance is $\mathbf{Z}_L = R_L + jX_L$. The phasor current flowing through the circuit is: $$\mathbf{I}_L = \frac{\mathbf{V}_{th}}{\mathbf{Z}_{th} + \mathbf{Z}_L} = \frac{V_{th}}{(R_{th} + R_L) + j(X_{th} + X_L)}$$ The magnitude of the load current is: $$I_L = |\mathbf{I}_L| = \frac{V_{th}}{\sqrt{(R_{th} + R_L)^2 + (X_{th} + X_L)^2}}$$ The active power dissipated exclusively in the resistive part of the load $R_L$ is: $$P_L = I_L^2 R_L = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2 + (X_{th} + X_L)^2}$$ To maximize $P_L$, we first optimize with respect to the reactive component $X_L$: $$\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial X_L} = -\frac{V_{th}^2 R_L \cdot 2(X_{th} + X_L)}{\left[ (R_{th} + R_L)^2 + (X_{th} + X_L)^2 \right]^2} = 0$$ $$(X_{th} + X_L) = 0 \implies X_L = -X_{th}$$ This condition means the load reactance must be equal in magnitude and opposite in sign to the internal source reactance (conjugate reactive cancellation, achieving circuit resonance where net loop reactance is zero). Substituting $X_L = -X_{th}$ into the power equation yields: $$P_L = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2}$$ Differentiating this residual expression with respect to $R_L$ and setting to zero yields the identical result as the DC case: $$\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial R_L} = 0 \implies R_L = R_{th}$$ Combining both conditions: $$\mathbf{Z}_L = R_L + jX_L = R_{th} - jX_{th} = \mathbf{Z}_{th}^*$$ Hence, maximum power transfer in an AC circuit occurs when the load impedance is the complex conjugate of the Thévenin source impedance ($\mathbf{Z}_L = \mathbf{Z}_{th}^*$). The maximum active power delivered to the load is: $$P_{L,\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}$$

Case C: AC Load with Variable Magnitude but Fixed Phase Angle ($\mathbf{Z}_L = Z_L \angle \theta_L$): When only the magnitude $Z_L$ can be adjusted while its power factor angle $\theta_L$ is constant, maximizing $P_L$ yields: $$Z_L = |\mathbf{Z}_{th}| = \sqrt{R_{th}^2 + X_{th}^2}$$ The load impedance magnitude must equal the magnitude of the Thévenin impedance.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Network theorems form the fundamental analytical toolkit for simplifying complex, multiloop electrical networks into manageable equivalent representations. In linear, bilateral lumped-parameter circuits, Thévenin's, Norton's, Superposition, and the Maximum Power Transfer theorems provide rigorous methodologies for evaluating branch currents, node voltages, and power dissipation.

1. Thévenin's and Norton's Theorems: - Thévenin's Theorem states that any linear, bilateral, active two-terminal network containing independent or dependent sources and impedances can be replaced at its terminal pair $(A-B)$ by an equivalent circuit consisting of a single ideal voltage source $\mathbf{V}_{th}$ in series with an equivalent impedance $\mathbf{Z}_{th}$. - The open-circuit voltage $\mathbf{V}_{th}$ is the potential difference appearing across terminals $A-B$ when the load impedance $\mathbf{Z}_L$ is disconnected ($\mathbf{V}_{th} = \mathbf{V}_{oc}$). - The Thévenin impedance $\mathbf{Z}_{th}$ is the impedance looking back into the de-energized network across terminals $A-B$ with all independent voltage sources replaced by short circuits ($V=0$) and all independent current sources replaced by open circuits ($I=0$), while retaining all dependent sources. - Norton's Theorem is the dual of Thévenin's theorem. It replaces the network by an ideal short-circuit current source $\mathbf{I}_N$ connected in parallel with an internal Norton admittance $\mathbf{Y}_N$ (or impedance $\mathbf{Z}_N$). - The Norton current is the current flowing through a direct short circuit placed across terminals $A-B$: $$\mathbf{I}_N = \mathbf{I}_{sc} = \frac{\mathbf{V}_{th}}{\mathbf{Z}_{th}}$$ - The Norton impedance equals the Thévenin impedance: $\mathbf{Z}_N = \mathbf{Z}_{th}$.

2. Superposition Theorem: The Superposition Theorem states that in any linear, bilateral electrical network energized by multiple independent sources, the resultant current or voltage in any branch is the algebraic (or phasor) sum of the currents or voltages produced by each independent source acting alone, with all other independent sources replaced by their respective internal impedances (independent voltage sources short-circuited and independent current sources open-circuited). Dependent sources are left unaltered throughout the decomposition. $$\mathbf{I}_k = \sum_{m=1}^{N_v} \mathbf{I}_{k, V_m} + \sum_{n=1}^{N_i} \mathbf{I}_{k, I_n}$$ Note that superposition applies strictly to linear responses (current and voltage) and cannot be applied directly to non-linear power quantities ($P \neq \sum P_i$ because $P \propto I^2$).

3. Maximum Power Transfer Theorem: The Maximum Power Transfer Theorem defines the necessary and sufficient conditions under which an active source network delivers maximum real active power $P_L$ to an adjustable load.

Case A: DC Source and Resistive Load ($R_L$ variable): Consider a DC network represented by its Thévenin equivalent $(V_{th}, R_{th})$ feeding a variable load resistor $R_L$. The loop current is: $$I_L = \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L}$$ The real power absorbed by the load is: $$P_L = I_L^2 R_L = \left( \frac{V_{th}}{R_{th} + R_L} \right)^2 R_L = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2}$$ To determine the condition for maximum load power, we take the first derivative of $P_L$ with respect to $R_L$ and set it to zero: $$\frac{dP_L}{dR_L} = V_{th}^2 \cdot \frac{(R_{th} + R_L)^2 \cdot 1 - R_L \cdot 2(R_{th} + R_L)}{(R_{th} + R_L)^4} = 0$$ $$(R_{th} + R_L) - 2 R_L = 0 \implies R_{th} - R_L = 0 \implies R_L = R_{th}$$ Checking the second derivative confirms a true maximum: $$\left. \frac{d^2 P_L}{dR_L^2} \right|_{R_L = R_{th}} = -\frac{V_{th}^2}{8 R_{th}^3} < 0$$ Thus, maximum power transfer in a DC circuit occurs when the load resistance equals the internal Thévenin resistance ($R_L = R_{th}$). Under this matched condition, the maximum power delivered is: $$P_{L,\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_{th}}{(R_{th} + R_{th})^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_{th}}{4 R_{th}^2} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}$$ The total power generated by the source is: $$P_{source} = V_{th} I_L = V_{th} \left( \frac{V_{th}}{2 R_{th}} \right) = \frac{V_{th}^2}{2 R_{th}}$$ The transmission efficiency at maximum power transfer is: $$\eta = \frac{P_{L,\max}}{P_{source}} \times 100\% = \frac{V_{th}^2 / (4 R_{th})}{V_{th}^2 / (2 R_{th})} \times 100\% = 50\%$$ Half the generated power is unavoidably dissipated as heat inside the internal source resistance $R_{th}$. Consequently, maximum power transfer is critical in communications and RF electronics (where signal strength is in microwatts/milliwatts and reflection must be eliminated), whereas power systems prioritize transmission efficiency ($\eta > 95\%$) by operating with source internal impedances far smaller than the load impedance ($R_{th} \ll R_L$).

Case B: AC Source and Complex Load Impedance ($\mathbf{Z}_L = R_L + jX_L$ with both $R_L$ and $X_L$ independently variable): Let the source network be represented by its AC Thévenin equivalent $\mathbf{V}_{th} = V_{th} \angle 0^\circ$ and $\mathbf{Z}_{th} = R_{th} + jX_{th}$. The load impedance is $\mathbf{Z}_L = R_L + jX_L$. The phasor current flowing through the circuit is: $$\mathbf{I}_L = \frac{\mathbf{V}_{th}}{\mathbf{Z}_{th} + \mathbf{Z}_L} = \frac{V_{th}}{(R_{th} + R_L) + j(X_{th} + X_L)}$$ The magnitude of the load current is: $$I_L = |\mathbf{I}_L| = \frac{V_{th}}{\sqrt{(R_{th} + R_L)^2 + (X_{th} + X_L)^2}}$$ The active power dissipated exclusively in the resistive part of the load $R_L$ is: $$P_L = I_L^2 R_L = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2 + (X_{th} + X_L)^2}$$ To maximize $P_L$, we first optimize with respect to the reactive component $X_L$: $$\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial X_L} = -\frac{V_{th}^2 R_L \cdot 2(X_{th} + X_L)}{\left[ (R_{th} + R_L)^2 + (X_{th} + X_L)^2 \right]^2} = 0$$ $$(X_{th} + X_L) = 0 \implies X_L = -X_{th}$$ This condition means the load reactance must be equal in magnitude and opposite in sign to the internal source reactance (conjugate reactive cancellation, achieving circuit resonance where net loop reactance is zero). Substituting $X_L = -X_{th}$ into the power equation yields: $$P_L = \frac{V_{th}^2 R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2}$$ Differentiating this residual expression with respect to $R_L$ and setting to zero yields the identical result as the DC case: $$\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial R_L} = 0 \implies R_L = R_{th}$$ Combining both conditions: $$\mathbf{Z}_L = R_L + jX_L = R_{th} - jX_{th} = \mathbf{Z}_{th}^*$$ Hence, maximum power transfer in an AC circuit occurs when the load impedance is the complex conjugate of the Thévenin source impedance ($\mathbf{Z}_L = \mathbf{Z}_{th}^*$). The maximum active power delivered to the load is: $$P_{L,\max} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4 R_{th}}$$

Case C: AC Load with Variable Magnitude but Fixed Phase Angle ($\mathbf{Z}_L = Z_L \angle \theta_L$): When only the magnitude $Z_L$ can be adjusted while its power factor angle $\theta_L$ is constant, maximizing $P_L$ yields: $$Z_L = |\mathbf{Z}_{th}| = \sqrt{R_{th}^2 + X_{th}^2}$$ The load impedance magnitude must equal the magnitude of the Thévenin impedance.

Question 2 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: COMP-Q2

Q2. Derive the transient current and voltage responses for series RL, RC, and series RLC circuits excited by a DC step input. Discuss overdamped, critically damped, and underdamped conditions. Q2. Derive the transient current and voltage responses for series RL, RC, and series RLC circuits excited by a DC step input. Discuss overdamped, critically damped, and underdamped conditions. Q2. Derive the transient current and voltage responses for series RL, RC, and series RLC circuits excited by a DC step input. Discuss overdamped, critically damped, and underdamped conditions.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R}(1 - e^{-t/\tau}), \ \tau = \frac{L}{R} \text{ or } RC, \quad s^2 + 2\zeta\omega_n s + \omega_n^2 = 0, \ \zeta = \frac{R}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C}{L}}$$

RL, RC, RLC Transients: First-order time constants are $\tau = L/R$ and $\tau = RC$. Second-order RLC circuit yields overdamped ($\zeta > 1$), critically damped ($\zeta = 1$), and underdamped oscillatory ($\zeta < 1$) responses with $\omega_d = \omega_n\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}$.

RL, RC, RLC Transients: First-order time constants are $\tau = L/R$ and $\tau = RC$. Second-order RLC circuit yields overdamped ($\zeta > 1$), critically damped ($\zeta = 1$), and underdamped oscillatory ($\zeta < 1$) responses with $\omega_d = \omega_n\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}$.

$$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R}(1 - e^{-t/\tau}), \ \tau = \frac{L}{R} \text{ or } RC, \quad s^2 + 2\zeta\omega_n s + \omega_n^2 = 0, \ \zeta = \frac{R}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C}{L}}$$

RL, RC, RLC Transients: First-order time constants are $\tau = L/R$ and $\tau = RC$. Second-order RLC circuit yields overdamped ($\zeta > 1$), critically damped ($\zeta = 1$), and underdamped oscillatory ($\zeta < 1$) responses with $\omega_d = \omega_n\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Electrical transients occur during transitions between distinct steady-state operating conditions following switching operations, faults, or sudden load changes. Energy-storing elements—inductors ($W_L = \frac{1}{2} L i^2$) and capacitors ($W_C = \frac{1}{2} C v^2$)—enforce boundary conditions preventing instantaneous changes in inductor current ($i_L(0^-) = i_L(0^+)$) and capacitor voltage ($v_C(0^-) = v_C(0^+)$).

1. Series RL Circuit with DC Step Excitation $V_s u(t)$: Consider an unenergized inductor $L$ in series with a resistor $R$ connected to a DC source $V_s$ at time $t=0$. Applying Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) for $t \ge 0$: $$R i(t) + L \frac{di(t)}{dt} = V_s$$ Rearranging into standard linear first-order differential form: $$\frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{R}{L} i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L}$$ The integrating factor is $I.F. = e^{\int \frac{R}{L} dt} = e^{\frac{R}{L}t}$. Multiplying both sides: $$\frac{d}{dt}\left[ i(t) e^{\frac{R}{L}t} \right] = \frac{V_s}{L} e^{\frac{R}{L}t}$$ Integrating with respect to $t$: $$i(t) e^{\frac{R}{L}t} = \frac{V_s}{L} \cdot \frac{L}{R} e^{\frac{R}{L}t} + K = \frac{V_s}{R} e^{\frac{R}{L}t} + K$$ $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R} + K e^{-\frac{R}{L}t}$$ Applying the initial boundary condition $i(0^+) = i(0^-) = 0$: $$0 = \frac{V_s}{R} + K \implies K = -\frac{V_s}{R}$$ Substituting $K$ yields the complete analytical solution: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R} \left( 1 - e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) \quad \text{for } t \ge 0$$ where the inductive time constant is defined as: $$\tau = \frac{L}{R} \quad \text{seconds}$$ The time constant $\tau$ represents the time required for the current to reach $1 - e^{-1} \approx 63.2\%$ of its steady-state final value $I_{ss} = \frac{V_s}{R}$. The induced inductor voltage is: $$v_L(t) = L \frac{di(t)}{dt} = L \cdot \frac{V_s}{R} \left( \frac{R}{L} e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) = V_s e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}}$$ At $t=0^+$, $v_L(0^+) = V_s$ (the inductor opposes current rise and acts as an open circuit). At $t \to \infty$, $v_L(\infty) = 0$ (the inductor acts as an ideal short circuit).

2. Series RC Circuit with DC Step Excitation $V_s u(t)$: For an uncharged capacitor $C$ in series with resistor $R$ energized by $V_s$ at $t=0$, KVL gives: $$R i(t) + v_C(t) = V_s \implies R C \frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} + v_C(t) = V_s$$ $$\frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{RC} v_C(t) = \frac{V_s}{RC}$$ Solving by the same integrating factor method with boundary condition $v_C(0^+) = v_C(0^-) = 0$: $$v_C(t) = V_s \left( 1 - e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) \quad \text{where } \tau = R C \text{ seconds}$$ The charging current is: $$i(t) = C \frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} = \frac{V_s}{R} e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}}$$ At $t=0^+$, $v_C(0^+) = 0$ and $i(0^+) = \frac{V_s}{R}$ (uncharged capacitor behaves as a short circuit). At $t \to \infty$, $v_C(\infty) = V_s$ and $i(\infty) = 0$ (fully charged capacitor behaves as an open circuit).

3. Series RLC Circuit Transient Analysis: Applying KVL to a series RLC loop energized by DC voltage $V_s$: $$R i(t) + L \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{C}\int_0^t i(\tau) d\tau + v_C(0^+) = V_s$$ Differentiating with respect to $t$: $$L \frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + R \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{C} i(t) = 0$$ Dividing by $L$: $$\frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + \frac{R}{L}\frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{LC} i(t) = 0$$ This is a homogeneous second-order linear differential equation with constant coefficients. Writing in standard second-order control/circuit notation: $$\frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + 2\alpha \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \omega_0^2 i(t) = 0$$ where: - Damping attenuation factor: $\alpha = \frac{R}{2L} \text{ rad/s}$ - Undamped resonant angular frequency: $\omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} \text{ rad/s}$ - Damping ratio: $\zeta = \frac{\alpha}{\omega_0} = \frac{R}{2L} \sqrt{LC} = \frac{R}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C}{L}}$

The characteristic algebraic equation is: $$s^2 + 2\alpha s + \omega_0^2 = 0 \implies s_{1,2} = -\alpha \pm \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}$$

Depending on the discriminant $\Delta = \alpha^2 - \omega_0^2$, three distinct physical regimes emerge:

Case 1: Overdamped System ($\alpha > \omega_0$, $\zeta > 1 \implies R > 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The characteristic roots are real, distinct, and negative: $$s_1 = -\alpha + \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}, \quad s_2 = -\alpha - \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}$$ The general solution for current is: $$i(t) = A_1 e^{s_1 t} + A_2 e^{s_2 t}$$ With initial conditions $i(0^+) = 0$ and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = \frac{V_s}{L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L(s_1 - s_2)}\left( e^{s_1 t} - e^{s_2 t} \right)$$ The response is purely non-oscillatory and sluggish; the capacitor charges slowly to $V_s$ without overshoot.

Case 2: Critically Damped System ($\alpha = \omega_0$, $\zeta = 1 \implies R = 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The characteristic roots are real, equal, and negative: $$s_1 = s_2 = -\alpha = -\frac{R}{2L}$$ The solution takes the repeated root form: $$i(t) = (A_1 + A_2 t) e^{-\alpha t}$$ Applying $i(0^+) = 0 \implies A_1 = 0$, and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = A_2 = \frac{V_s}{L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L} t e^{-\frac{R}{2L}t}$$ Critical damping provides the fastest possible monotonic transition to steady-state without producing any oscillatory overshoot.

Case 3: Underdamped System ($\alpha < \omega_0$, $\zeta < 1 \implies R < 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The roots are complex conjugate pairs located in the left half of the s-plane: $$s_{1,2} = -\alpha \pm j \omega_d \quad \text{where } \omega_d = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \alpha^2} = \omega_0 \sqrt{1 - \zeta^2}$$ The current response is an exponentially damped sinusoidal oscillation: $$i(t) = e^{-\alpha t} \left( B_1 \cos\omega_d t + B_2 \sin\omega_d t \right)$$ Using $i(0^+) = 0 \implies B_1 = 0$, and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = \omega_d B_2 = \frac{V_s}{L} \implies B_2 = \frac{V_s}{\omega_d L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{\omega_d L} e^{-\alpha t} \sin\omega_d t$$ The capacitor voltage is: $$v_C(t) = V_s - e^{-\alpha t}\left[ V_s \cos\omega_d t + \frac{\alpha V_s}{\omega_d}\sin\omega_d t \right] = V_s \left[ 1 - \frac{\omega_0}{\omega_d} e^{-\alpha t} \sin(\omega_d t + \theta) \right]$$ where $\theta = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\omega_d}{\alpha}\right) = \cos^{-1}\zeta$. The capacitor voltage overshoots $V_s$, reaching a peak of up to $2 V_s$ when damping is negligible ($R \to 0$).

Electrical transients occur during transitions between distinct steady-state operating conditions following switching operations, faults, or sudden load changes. Energy-storing elements—inductors ($W_L = \frac{1}{2} L i^2$) and capacitors ($W_C = \frac{1}{2} C v^2$)—enforce boundary conditions preventing instantaneous changes in inductor current ($i_L(0^-) = i_L(0^+)$) and capacitor voltage ($v_C(0^-) = v_C(0^+)$).

1. Series RL Circuit with DC Step Excitation $V_s u(t)$: Consider an unenergized inductor $L$ in series with a resistor $R$ connected to a DC source $V_s$ at time $t=0$. Applying Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) for $t \ge 0$: $$R i(t) + L \frac{di(t)}{dt} = V_s$$ Rearranging into standard linear first-order differential form: $$\frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{R}{L} i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L}$$ The integrating factor is $I.F. = e^{\int \frac{R}{L} dt} = e^{\frac{R}{L}t}$. Multiplying both sides: $$\frac{d}{dt}\left[ i(t) e^{\frac{R}{L}t} \right] = \frac{V_s}{L} e^{\frac{R}{L}t}$$ Integrating with respect to $t$: $$i(t) e^{\frac{R}{L}t} = \frac{V_s}{L} \cdot \frac{L}{R} e^{\frac{R}{L}t} + K = \frac{V_s}{R} e^{\frac{R}{L}t} + K$$ $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R} + K e^{-\frac{R}{L}t}$$ Applying the initial boundary condition $i(0^+) = i(0^-) = 0$: $$0 = \frac{V_s}{R} + K \implies K = -\frac{V_s}{R}$$ Substituting $K$ yields the complete analytical solution: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R} \left( 1 - e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) \quad \text{for } t \ge 0$$ where the inductive time constant is defined as: $$\tau = \frac{L}{R} \quad \text{seconds}$$ The time constant $\tau$ represents the time required for the current to reach $1 - e^{-1} \approx 63.2\%$ of its steady-state final value $I_{ss} = \frac{V_s}{R}$. The induced inductor voltage is: $$v_L(t) = L \frac{di(t)}{dt} = L \cdot \frac{V_s}{R} \left( \frac{R}{L} e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) = V_s e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}}$$ At $t=0^+$, $v_L(0^+) = V_s$ (the inductor opposes current rise and acts as an open circuit). At $t \to \infty$, $v_L(\infty) = 0$ (the inductor acts as an ideal short circuit).

2. Series RC Circuit with DC Step Excitation $V_s u(t)$: For an uncharged capacitor $C$ in series with resistor $R$ energized by $V_s$ at $t=0$, KVL gives: $$R i(t) + v_C(t) = V_s \implies R C \frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} + v_C(t) = V_s$$ $$\frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{RC} v_C(t) = \frac{V_s}{RC}$$ Solving by the same integrating factor method with boundary condition $v_C(0^+) = v_C(0^-) = 0$: $$v_C(t) = V_s \left( 1 - e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) \quad \text{where } \tau = R C \text{ seconds}$$ The charging current is: $$i(t) = C \frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} = \frac{V_s}{R} e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}}$$ At $t=0^+$, $v_C(0^+) = 0$ and $i(0^+) = \frac{V_s}{R}$ (uncharged capacitor behaves as a short circuit). At $t \to \infty$, $v_C(\infty) = V_s$ and $i(\infty) = 0$ (fully charged capacitor behaves as an open circuit).

3. Series RLC Circuit Transient Analysis: Applying KVL to a series RLC loop energized by DC voltage $V_s$: $$R i(t) + L \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{C}\int_0^t i(\tau) d\tau + v_C(0^+) = V_s$$ Differentiating with respect to $t$: $$L \frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + R \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{C} i(t) = 0$$ Dividing by $L$: $$\frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + \frac{R}{L}\frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{LC} i(t) = 0$$ This is a homogeneous second-order linear differential equation with constant coefficients. Writing in standard second-order control/circuit notation: $$\frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + 2\alpha \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \omega_0^2 i(t) = 0$$ where: - Damping attenuation factor: $\alpha = \frac{R}{2L} \text{ rad/s}$ - Undamped resonant angular frequency: $\omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} \text{ rad/s}$ - Damping ratio: $\zeta = \frac{\alpha}{\omega_0} = \frac{R}{2L} \sqrt{LC} = \frac{R}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C}{L}}$

The characteristic algebraic equation is: $$s^2 + 2\alpha s + \omega_0^2 = 0 \implies s_{1,2} = -\alpha \pm \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}$$

Depending on the discriminant $\Delta = \alpha^2 - \omega_0^2$, three distinct physical regimes emerge:

Case 1: Overdamped System ($\alpha > \omega_0$, $\zeta > 1 \implies R > 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The characteristic roots are real, distinct, and negative: $$s_1 = -\alpha + \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}, \quad s_2 = -\alpha - \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}$$ The general solution for current is: $$i(t) = A_1 e^{s_1 t} + A_2 e^{s_2 t}$$ With initial conditions $i(0^+) = 0$ and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = \frac{V_s}{L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L(s_1 - s_2)}\left( e^{s_1 t} - e^{s_2 t} \right)$$ The response is purely non-oscillatory and sluggish; the capacitor charges slowly to $V_s$ without overshoot.

Case 2: Critically Damped System ($\alpha = \omega_0$, $\zeta = 1 \implies R = 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The characteristic roots are real, equal, and negative: $$s_1 = s_2 = -\alpha = -\frac{R}{2L}$$ The solution takes the repeated root form: $$i(t) = (A_1 + A_2 t) e^{-\alpha t}$$ Applying $i(0^+) = 0 \implies A_1 = 0$, and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = A_2 = \frac{V_s}{L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L} t e^{-\frac{R}{2L}t}$$ Critical damping provides the fastest possible monotonic transition to steady-state without producing any oscillatory overshoot.

Case 3: Underdamped System ($\alpha < \omega_0$, $\zeta < 1 \implies R < 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The roots are complex conjugate pairs located in the left half of the s-plane: $$s_{1,2} = -\alpha \pm j \omega_d \quad \text{where } \omega_d = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \alpha^2} = \omega_0 \sqrt{1 - \zeta^2}$$ The current response is an exponentially damped sinusoidal oscillation: $$i(t) = e^{-\alpha t} \left( B_1 \cos\omega_d t + B_2 \sin\omega_d t \right)$$ Using $i(0^+) = 0 \implies B_1 = 0$, and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = \omega_d B_2 = \frac{V_s}{L} \implies B_2 = \frac{V_s}{\omega_d L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{\omega_d L} e^{-\alpha t} \sin\omega_d t$$ The capacitor voltage is: $$v_C(t) = V_s - e^{-\alpha t}\left[ V_s \cos\omega_d t + \frac{\alpha V_s}{\omega_d}\sin\omega_d t \right] = V_s \left[ 1 - \frac{\omega_0}{\omega_d} e^{-\alpha t} \sin(\omega_d t + \theta) \right]$$ where $\theta = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\omega_d}{\alpha}\right) = \cos^{-1}\zeta$. The capacitor voltage overshoots $V_s$, reaching a peak of up to $2 V_s$ when damping is negligible ($R \to 0$).

Electrical transients occur during transitions between distinct steady-state operating conditions following switching operations, faults, or sudden load changes. Energy-storing elements—inductors ($W_L = \frac{1}{2} L i^2$) and capacitors ($W_C = \frac{1}{2} C v^2$)—enforce boundary conditions preventing instantaneous changes in inductor current ($i_L(0^-) = i_L(0^+)$) and capacitor voltage ($v_C(0^-) = v_C(0^+)$).

1. Series RL Circuit with DC Step Excitation $V_s u(t)$: Consider an unenergized inductor $L$ in series with a resistor $R$ connected to a DC source $V_s$ at time $t=0$. Applying Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) for $t \ge 0$: $$R i(t) + L \frac{di(t)}{dt} = V_s$$ Rearranging into standard linear first-order differential form: $$\frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{R}{L} i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L}$$ The integrating factor is $I.F. = e^{\int \frac{R}{L} dt} = e^{\frac{R}{L}t}$. Multiplying both sides: $$\frac{d}{dt}\left[ i(t) e^{\frac{R}{L}t} \right] = \frac{V_s}{L} e^{\frac{R}{L}t}$$ Integrating with respect to $t$: $$i(t) e^{\frac{R}{L}t} = \frac{V_s}{L} \cdot \frac{L}{R} e^{\frac{R}{L}t} + K = \frac{V_s}{R} e^{\frac{R}{L}t} + K$$ $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R} + K e^{-\frac{R}{L}t}$$ Applying the initial boundary condition $i(0^+) = i(0^-) = 0$: $$0 = \frac{V_s}{R} + K \implies K = -\frac{V_s}{R}$$ Substituting $K$ yields the complete analytical solution: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R} \left( 1 - e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) \quad \text{for } t \ge 0$$ where the inductive time constant is defined as: $$\tau = \frac{L}{R} \quad \text{seconds}$$ The time constant $\tau$ represents the time required for the current to reach $1 - e^{-1} \approx 63.2\%$ of its steady-state final value $I_{ss} = \frac{V_s}{R}$. The induced inductor voltage is: $$v_L(t) = L \frac{di(t)}{dt} = L \cdot \frac{V_s}{R} \left( \frac{R}{L} e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) = V_s e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}}$$ At $t=0^+$, $v_L(0^+) = V_s$ (the inductor opposes current rise and acts as an open circuit). At $t \to \infty$, $v_L(\infty) = 0$ (the inductor acts as an ideal short circuit).

2. Series RC Circuit with DC Step Excitation $V_s u(t)$: For an uncharged capacitor $C$ in series with resistor $R$ energized by $V_s$ at $t=0$, KVL gives: $$R i(t) + v_C(t) = V_s \implies R C \frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} + v_C(t) = V_s$$ $$\frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{RC} v_C(t) = \frac{V_s}{RC}$$ Solving by the same integrating factor method with boundary condition $v_C(0^+) = v_C(0^-) = 0$: $$v_C(t) = V_s \left( 1 - e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}} \right) \quad \text{where } \tau = R C \text{ seconds}$$ The charging current is: $$i(t) = C \frac{dv_C(t)}{dt} = \frac{V_s}{R} e^{-\frac{t}{\tau}}$$ At $t=0^+$, $v_C(0^+) = 0$ and $i(0^+) = \frac{V_s}{R}$ (uncharged capacitor behaves as a short circuit). At $t \to \infty$, $v_C(\infty) = V_s$ and $i(\infty) = 0$ (fully charged capacitor behaves as an open circuit).

3. Series RLC Circuit Transient Analysis: Applying KVL to a series RLC loop energized by DC voltage $V_s$: $$R i(t) + L \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{C}\int_0^t i(\tau) d\tau + v_C(0^+) = V_s$$ Differentiating with respect to $t$: $$L \frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + R \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{C} i(t) = 0$$ Dividing by $L$: $$\frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + \frac{R}{L}\frac{di(t)}{dt} + \frac{1}{LC} i(t) = 0$$ This is a homogeneous second-order linear differential equation with constant coefficients. Writing in standard second-order control/circuit notation: $$\frac{d^2 i(t)}{dt^2} + 2\alpha \frac{di(t)}{dt} + \omega_0^2 i(t) = 0$$ where: - Damping attenuation factor: $\alpha = \frac{R}{2L} \text{ rad/s}$ - Undamped resonant angular frequency: $\omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} \text{ rad/s}$ - Damping ratio: $\zeta = \frac{\alpha}{\omega_0} = \frac{R}{2L} \sqrt{LC} = \frac{R}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C}{L}}$

The characteristic algebraic equation is: $$s^2 + 2\alpha s + \omega_0^2 = 0 \implies s_{1,2} = -\alpha \pm \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}$$

Depending on the discriminant $\Delta = \alpha^2 - \omega_0^2$, three distinct physical regimes emerge:

Case 1: Overdamped System ($\alpha > \omega_0$, $\zeta > 1 \implies R > 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The characteristic roots are real, distinct, and negative: $$s_1 = -\alpha + \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}, \quad s_2 = -\alpha - \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}$$ The general solution for current is: $$i(t) = A_1 e^{s_1 t} + A_2 e^{s_2 t}$$ With initial conditions $i(0^+) = 0$ and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = \frac{V_s}{L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L(s_1 - s_2)}\left( e^{s_1 t} - e^{s_2 t} \right)$$ The response is purely non-oscillatory and sluggish; the capacitor charges slowly to $V_s$ without overshoot.

Case 2: Critically Damped System ($\alpha = \omega_0$, $\zeta = 1 \implies R = 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The characteristic roots are real, equal, and negative: $$s_1 = s_2 = -\alpha = -\frac{R}{2L}$$ The solution takes the repeated root form: $$i(t) = (A_1 + A_2 t) e^{-\alpha t}$$ Applying $i(0^+) = 0 \implies A_1 = 0$, and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = A_2 = \frac{V_s}{L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{L} t e^{-\frac{R}{2L}t}$$ Critical damping provides the fastest possible monotonic transition to steady-state without producing any oscillatory overshoot.

Case 3: Underdamped System ($\alpha < \omega_0$, $\zeta < 1 \implies R < 2\sqrt{L/C}$): The roots are complex conjugate pairs located in the left half of the s-plane: $$s_{1,2} = -\alpha \pm j \omega_d \quad \text{where } \omega_d = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \alpha^2} = \omega_0 \sqrt{1 - \zeta^2}$$ The current response is an exponentially damped sinusoidal oscillation: $$i(t) = e^{-\alpha t} \left( B_1 \cos\omega_d t + B_2 \sin\omega_d t \right)$$ Using $i(0^+) = 0 \implies B_1 = 0$, and $\left.\frac{di}{dt}\right|_{0^+} = \omega_d B_2 = \frac{V_s}{L} \implies B_2 = \frac{V_s}{\omega_d L}$: $$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{\omega_d L} e^{-\alpha t} \sin\omega_d t$$ The capacitor voltage is: $$v_C(t) = V_s - e^{-\alpha t}\left[ V_s \cos\omega_d t + \frac{\alpha V_s}{\omega_d}\sin\omega_d t \right] = V_s \left[ 1 - \frac{\omega_0}{\omega_d} e^{-\alpha t} \sin(\omega_d t + \theta) \right]$$ where $\theta = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\omega_d}{\alpha}\right) = \cos^{-1}\zeta$. The capacitor voltage overshoots $V_s$, reaching a peak of up to $2 V_s$ when damping is negligible ($R \to 0$).

Question 3 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: COMP-Q3

Q3. Derive the resonant frequency, half-power bandwidth, and Quality factor for a series RLC circuit. Explain parallel resonance in a practical tank circuit and derive its dynamic impedance. Q3. Derive the resonant frequency, half-power bandwidth, and Quality factor for a series RLC circuit. Explain parallel resonance in a practical tank circuit and derive its dynamic impedance. Q3. Derive the resonant frequency, half-power bandwidth, and Quality factor for a series RLC circuit. Explain parallel resonance in a practical tank circuit and derive its dynamic impedance.

🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (EET308) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 12(a) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}, \quad Q = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R} = \frac{1}{R}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}, \quad BW = \frac{\omega_0}{Q} = \frac{R}{L}, \quad Z_d = \frac{L}{C R_L}$$

AC Resonance: Series resonance yields minimum impedance $Z_0 = R$ and maximum current with voltage magnification $Q V$. Parallel tank circuit yields maximum dynamic impedance $Z_d = L/(C R_L)$ (anti-resonance) and minimum line current.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}, \quad Q = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R} = \frac{1}{R}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}, \quad BW = \frac{\omega_0}{Q} = \frac{R}{L}, \quad Z_d = \frac{L}{C R_L}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}, \quad Q = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R} = \frac{1}{R}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}, \quad BW = \frac{\omega_0}{Q} = \frac{R}{L}, \quad Z_d = \frac{L}{C R_L}$$

AC Resonance: Series resonance yields minimum impedance $Z_0 = R$ and maximum current with voltage magnification $Q V$. Parallel tank circuit yields maximum dynamic impedance $Z_d = L/(C R_L)$ (anti-resonance) and minimum line current.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Resonance in an AC circuit occurs at a specific excitation frequency at which the inductive reactance and capacitive reactance cancel each other out, making the net circuit impedance purely resistive. Under resonance, the input voltage and total input current are exactly in phase, resulting in a unity power factor ($\cos\phi = 1.0$).

1. Series RLC Resonance: Consider an inductor $L$, capacitor $C$, and resistor $R$ connected in series across an AC source $\mathbf{V} = V \angle 0^\circ$ of variable angular frequency $\omega$. The total complex input impedance is: $$\mathbf{Z}(j\omega) = R + j\left( \omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} \right)$$ The condition for resonance is that the imaginary reactive part of $\mathbf{Z}(j\omega)$ becomes identically zero: $$\text{Im}[\mathbf{Z}(j\omega_0)] = \omega_0 L - \frac{1}{\omega_0 C} = 0 \implies \omega_0^2 = \frac{1}{LC}$$ $$\omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} \text{ rad/s} \quad \implies \quad f_0 = \frac{1}{2\pi \sqrt{LC}} \text{ Hz}$$

Characteristics of Series Resonance: - Impedance is at its absolute minimum: $\mathbf{Z}_0 = R$. - Current is at its absolute maximum: $\mathbf{I}_0 = \frac{\mathbf{V}}{R} \angle 0^\circ$. - Power factor is strictly unity ($\cos\phi = 1.0$). - Voltage Magnification: Although the net reactive voltage is zero ($V_L + V_C = 0$), individual voltages across $L$ and $C$ can be dangerously higher than the applied supply voltage $V$: $$V_L = I_0 \omega_0 L = \frac{V}{R} \omega_0 L = Q \cdot V$$ $$V_C = \frac{I_0}{\omega_0 C} = \frac{V}{R \omega_0 C} = Q \cdot V$$ where $Q$ is the Quality Factor of the resonant circuit.

2. Quality Factor ($Q$) and Bandwidth ($BW$): The Quality Factor $Q$ is defined physically as $2\pi$ times the ratio of maximum energy stored in the circuit to the energy dissipated per cycle: $$Q = 2\pi \frac{\text{Maximum Energy Stored}}{\text{Energy Dissipated per Cycle}} = 2\pi \frac{\frac{1}{2} L I_0^2}{I_{rms}^2 R \cdot T} = 2\pi \frac{\frac{1}{2} L I_0^2}{\frac{1}{2} I_0^2 R (2\pi / \omega_0)} = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R}$$ Substituting $\omega_0 = 1/\sqrt{LC}$: $$Q = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R} = \frac{1}{\omega_0 C R} = \frac{1}{R}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}$$

Half-Power Frequencies ($\omega_1, \omega_2$): The half-power frequencies (or $3\text{ dB}$ cutoff frequencies) occur when the active power delivered to the circuit drops to half of its maximum resonant value ($P = \frac{1}{2} P_{\max}$), which corresponds to the current falling to $I = \frac{I_0}{\sqrt{2}} \approx 0.707 I_0$. Setting $|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{2} R$: $$\sqrt{R^2 + \left( \omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} \right)^2} = \sqrt{2} R \implies \left( \omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} \right)^2 = R^2$$ $$\omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} = \pm R$$ - For lower cutoff frequency $\omega_1$: $\frac{1}{\omega_1 C} - \omega_1 L = R \implies \omega_1^2 + \frac{R}{L}\omega_1 - \frac{1}{LC} = 0$ $$\omega_1 = -\frac{R}{2L} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{R}{2L}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{LC}}$$ - For upper cutoff frequency $\omega_2$: $\omega_2 L - \frac{1}{\omega_2 C} = R \implies \omega_2^2 - \frac{R}{L}\omega_2 - \frac{1}{LC} = 0$ $$\omega_2 = +\frac{R}{2L} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{R}{2L}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{LC}}$$

The Bandwidth ($BW$) is the difference between upper and lower half-power frequencies: $$BW = \Delta\omega = \omega_2 - \omega_1 = \frac{R}{L} \text{ rad/s} \quad \implies \quad BW = \Delta f = \frac{R}{2\pi L} \text{ Hz}$$ Dividing $\omega_0$ by $BW$: $$\frac{\omega_0}{BW} = \frac{\omega_0}{R/L} = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R} = Q \implies BW = \frac{\omega_0}{Q} = \frac{f_0}{Q}$$ The resonant frequency is the geometric mean of the half-power frequencies: $$\omega_0 = \sqrt{\omega_1 \omega_2}$$ Selectivity is inversely proportional to bandwidth: a high-$Q$ circuit exhibits a narrow bandwidth, offering high selectivity to isolate specific frequency signals (essential in RF tuning).

3. Parallel AC Resonance (Practical Tank Circuit): Consider a practical inductor with internal winding resistance $R_L$ and inductance $L$ connected in parallel with a pure capacitor $C$. The total circuit admittance is: $$\mathbf{Y}(j\omega) = \mathbf{Y}_L + \mathbf{Y}_C = \frac{1}{R_L + j\omega L} + j\omega C = \frac{R_L - j\omega L}{R_L^2 + \omega^2 L^2} + j\omega C$$ $$\mathbf{Y}(j\omega) = \frac{R_L}{R_L^2 + \omega^2 L^2} + j\left( \omega C - \frac{\omega L}{R_L^2 + \omega^2 L^2} \right)$$ At parallel resonance, the circuit exhibits unity power factor, which requires the imaginary susceptance to vanish: $$\omega_r C - \frac{\omega_r L}{R_L^2 + \omega_r^2 L^2} = 0 \implies C = \frac{L}{R_L^2 + \omega_r^2 L^2}$$ $$R_L^2 + \omega_r^2 L^2 = \frac{L}{C} \implies \omega_r^2 L^2 = \frac{L}{C} - R_L^2$$ Dividing by $L^2$: $$\omega_r = \sqrt{\frac{1}{LC} - \frac{R_L^2}{L^2}} \text{ rad/s}$$ Note that for resonance to exist in a practical tank circuit, the condition $L/C > R_L^2$ must be satisfied. If $R_L \ll \omega L$ (ideal coil), $\omega_r \approx \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} = \omega_0$.

Dynamic Impedance ($Z_d$) at Parallel Resonance: At resonance, the susceptance is zero, leaving only the conductance: $$\mathbf{Y}_r = \frac{R_L}{R_L^2 + \omega_r^2 L^2} = \frac{R_L}{L/C} = \frac{C R_L}{L}$$ The equivalent dynamic impedance $Z_d$ of the parallel tank at resonance is: $$Z_d = \frac{1}{\mathbf{Y}_r} = \frac{L}{C R_L}$$ Since $R_L$ is generally small, $Z_d$ is extremely large (anti-resonance). Thus: - Parallel resonance exhibits maximum impedance $Z_d = \frac{L}{C R_L}$ and minimum line current $I_{line} = \frac{V}{Z_d}$. - Circulating tank currents inside the $L-C$ loop are magnified: $I_{circ} = Q \cdot I_{line}$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Resonance in an AC circuit occurs at a specific excitation frequency at which the inductive reactance and capacitive reactance cancel each other out, making the net circuit impedance purely resistive. Under resonance, the input voltage and total input current are exactly in phase, resulting in a unity power factor ($\cos\phi = 1.0$).

1. Series RLC Resonance: Consider an inductor $L$, capacitor $C$, and resistor $R$ connected in series across an AC source $\mathbf{V} = V \angle 0^\circ$ of variable angular frequency $\omega$. The total complex input impedance is: $$\mathbf{Z}(j\omega) = R + j\left( \omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} \right)$$ The condition for resonance is that the imaginary reactive part of $\mathbf{Z}(j\omega)$ becomes identically zero: $$\text{Im}[\mathbf{Z}(j\omega_0)] = \omega_0 L - \frac{1}{\omega_0 C} = 0 \implies \omega_0^2 = \frac{1}{LC}$$ $$\omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} \text{ rad/s} \quad \implies \quad f_0 = \frac{1}{2\pi \sqrt{LC}} \text{ Hz}$$

Characteristics of Series Resonance: - Impedance is at its absolute minimum: $\mathbf{Z}_0 = R$. - Current is at its absolute maximum: $\mathbf{I}_0 = \frac{\mathbf{V}}{R} \angle 0^\circ$. - Power factor is strictly unity ($\cos\phi = 1.0$). - Voltage Magnification: Although the net reactive voltage is zero ($V_L + V_C = 0$), individual voltages across $L$ and $C$ can be dangerously higher than the applied supply voltage $V$: $$V_L = I_0 \omega_0 L = \frac{V}{R} \omega_0 L = Q \cdot V$$ $$V_C = \frac{I_0}{\omega_0 C} = \frac{V}{R \omega_0 C} = Q \cdot V$$ where $Q$ is the Quality Factor of the resonant circuit.

2. Quality Factor ($Q$) and Bandwidth ($BW$): The Quality Factor $Q$ is defined physically as $2\pi$ times the ratio of maximum energy stored in the circuit to the energy dissipated per cycle: $$Q = 2\pi \frac{\text{Maximum Energy Stored}}{\text{Energy Dissipated per Cycle}} = 2\pi \frac{\frac{1}{2} L I_0^2}{I_{rms}^2 R \cdot T} = 2\pi \frac{\frac{1}{2} L I_0^2}{\frac{1}{2} I_0^2 R (2\pi / \omega_0)} = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R}$$ Substituting $\omega_0 = 1/\sqrt{LC}$: $$Q = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R} = \frac{1}{\omega_0 C R} = \frac{1}{R}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}$$

Half-Power Frequencies ($\omega_1, \omega_2$): The half-power frequencies (or $3\text{ dB}$ cutoff frequencies) occur when the active power delivered to the circuit drops to half of its maximum resonant value ($P = \frac{1}{2} P_{\max}$), which corresponds to the current falling to $I = \frac{I_0}{\sqrt{2}} \approx 0.707 I_0$. Setting $|\mathbf{Z}| = \sqrt{2} R$: $$\sqrt{R^2 + \left( \omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} \right)^2} = \sqrt{2} R \implies \left( \omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} \right)^2 = R^2$$ $$\omega L - \frac{1}{\omega C} = \pm R$$ - For lower cutoff frequency $\omega_1$: $\frac{1}{\omega_1 C} - \omega_1 L = R \implies \omega_1^2 + \frac{R}{L}\omega_1 - \frac{1}{LC} = 0$ $$\omega_1 = -\frac{R}{2L} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{R}{2L}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{LC}}$$ - For upper cutoff frequency $\omega_2$: $\omega_2 L - \frac{1}{\omega_2 C} = R \implies \omega_2^2 - \frac{R}{L}\omega_2 - \frac{1}{LC} = 0$ $$\omega_2 = +\frac{R}{2L} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{R}{2L}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{LC}}$$

The Bandwidth ($BW$) is the difference between upper and lower half-power frequencies: $$BW = \Delta\omega = \omega_2 - \omega_1 = \frac{R}{L} \text{ rad/s} \quad \implies \quad BW = \Delta f = \frac{R}{2\pi L} \text{ Hz}$$ Dividing $\omega_0$ by $BW$: $$\frac{\omega_0}{BW} = \frac{\omega_0}{R/L} = \frac{\omega_0 L}{R} = Q \implies BW = \frac{\omega_0}{Q} = \frac{f_0}{Q}$$ The resonant frequency is the geometric mean of the half-power frequencies: $$\omega_0 = \sqrt{\omega_1 \omega_2}$$ Selectivity is inversely proportional to bandwidth: a high-$Q$ circuit exhibits a narrow bandwidth, offering high selectivity to isolate specific frequency signals (essential in RF tuning).

3. Parallel AC Resonance (Practical Tank Circuit): Consider a practical inductor with internal winding resistance $R_L$ and inductance $L$ connected in parallel with a pure capacitor $C$. The total circuit admittance is: $$\mathbf{Y}(j\omega) = \mathbf{Y}_L + \mathbf{Y}_C = \frac{1}{R_L + j\omega L} + j\omega C = \frac{R_L - j\omega L}{R_L^2 + \omega^2 L^2} + j\omega C$$ $$\mathbf{Y}(j\omega) = \frac{R_L}{R_L^2 + \omega^2 L^2} + j\left( \omega C - \frac{\omega L}{R_L^2 + \omega^2 L^2} \right)$$ At parallel resonance, the circuit exhibits unity power factor, which requires the imaginary susceptance to vanish: $$\omega_r C - \frac{\omega_r L}{R_L^2 + \omega_r^2 L^2} = 0 \implies C = \frac{L}{R_L^2 + \omega_r^2 L^2}$$ $$R_L^2 + \omega_r^2 L^2 = \frac{L}{C} \implies \omega_r^2 L^2 = \frac{L}{C} - R_L^2$$ Dividing by $L^2$: $$\omega_r = \sqrt{\frac{1}{LC} - \frac{R_L^2}{L^2}} \text{ rad/s}$$ Note that for resonance to exist in a practical tank circuit, the condition $L/C > R_L^2$ must be satisfied. If $R_L \ll \omega L$ (ideal coil), $\omega_r \approx \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} = \omega_0$.

Dynamic Impedance ($Z_d$) at Parallel Resonance: At resonance, the susceptance is zero, leaving only the conductance: $$\mathbf{Y}_r = \frac{R_L}{R_L^2 + \omega_r^2 L^2} = \frac{R_L}{L/C} = \frac{C R_L}{L}$$ The equivalent dynamic impedance $Z_d$ of the parallel tank at resonance is: $$Z_d = \frac{1}{\mathbf{Y}_r} = \frac{L}{C R_L}$$ Since $R_L$ is generally small, $Z_d$ is extremely large (anti-resonance). Thus: - Parallel resonance exhibits maximum impedance $Z_d = \frac{L}{C R_L}$ and minimum line current $I_{line} = \frac{V}{Z_d}$. - Circulating tank currents inside the $L-C$ loop are magnified: $I_{circ} = Q \cdot I_{line}$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Resonance in an AC circuit occurs at a specific excitation frequency at which the inductive reactance and capacitive reactance cancel each other out, making the net circuit impedance purely resistive. Under resonance, the input voltage and total input current are exactly in phase, resulting in a unity power factor ($\cos\phi = 1.0$).

3. Parallel AC Resonance (Practical Tank Circuit): Consider a practical inductor with internal winding resistance $R_L$ and inductance $L$ connected in parallel with a pure capacitor $C$. Question 4 7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: COMP-Q4

Q4. Derive the relationships between line and phase voltages and currents in balanced Star and Delta AC systems. Explain the two-wattmeter method of 3-phase power measurement and derive the power factor formula. Q4. Derive the relationships between line and phase voltages and currents in balanced Star and Delta AC systems. Explain the two-wattmeter method of 3-phase power measurement and derive the power factor formula. Q4. Derive the relationships between line and phase voltages and currents in balanced Star and Delta AC systems. Explain the two-wattmeter method of 3-phase power measurement and derive the power factor formula.

Watch Video Solution
🏛️
KTU Exam Source: June 2023 University Exam (EET308) • Original Paper Location: Part B • Question 12(b) (7 Marks)
👁️ View Question Paper
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_L = \sqrt{3}V_{ph}, \quad P = W_1 + W_2 = \sqrt{3}V_L I_L \cos\phi, \quad \tan\phi = \sqrt{3}\frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2}$$

Three-Phase & Two-Wattmeter Method: Total 3-phase active power is the algebraic sum $W_1 + W_2$. Load power factor is derived from $\tan\phi = \sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2)/(W_1 + W_2)$. When $\cos\phi = 0.5$, $W_2 = 0$; when $\cos\phi < 0.5$, $W_2$ deflects negative.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_L = \sqrt{3}V_{ph}, \quad P = W_1 + W_2 = \sqrt{3}V_L I_L \cos\phi, \quad \tan\phi = \sqrt{3}\frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2}$$

Three-Phase & Two-Wattmeter Method: Total 3-phase active power is the algebraic sum $W_1 + W_2$. Load power factor is derived from $\tan\phi = \sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2)/(W_1 + W_2)$. When $\cos\phi = 0.5$, $W_2 = 0$; when $\cos\phi < 0.5$, $W_2$ deflects negative.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_L = \sqrt{3}V_{ph}, \quad P = W_1 + W_2 = \sqrt{3}V_L I_L \cos\phi, \quad \tan\phi = \sqrt{3}\frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2}$$

Three-Phase & Two-Wattmeter Method: Total 3-phase active power is the algebraic sum $W_1 + W_2$. Load power factor is derived from $\tan\phi = \sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2)/(W_1 + W_2)$. When $\cos\phi = 0.5$, $W_2 = 0$; when $\cos\phi < 0.5$, $W_2$ deflects negative.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Polyphase systems, predominantly balanced three-phase AC systems, provide constant instantaneous power delivery, higher generator utilization, and reduced copper volume in transmission lines compared to single-phase systems.

1. Star (Y) Connection Relationships: In a balanced star connection, the three line terminals $R, Y, B$ are fed from phase windings meeting at a common neutral point $N$. - Line currents equal phase currents: $$I_L = I_{ph}$$ - The line voltage is the phasor difference between two phase voltages: $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = \mathbf{V}_{RN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN}$$ With balanced phase voltages $\mathbf{V}_{RN} = V_{ph} \angle 0^\circ$, $\mathbf{V}_{YN} = V_{ph} \angle -120^\circ$, $\mathbf{V}_{BN} = V_{ph} \angle -240^\circ$: $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = V_{ph} (1 - \cos(-120^\circ) - j\sin(-120^\circ)) = V_{ph}\left( 1 - \left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) + j\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right) = V_{ph}\left( \frac{3}{2} + j\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right)$$ $$|\mathbf{V}_{RY}| = V_{ph} \sqrt{\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)^2} = V_{ph} \sqrt{\frac{9}{4} + \frac{3}{4}} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph}$$ The phase angle is $\tan^{-1}\left( \frac{\sqrt{3}/2}{3/2} \right) = 30^\circ$. Thus: $$V_L = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \quad (\text{Line voltage leads phase voltage by } 30^\circ)$$

2. Delta ($\Delta$) Connection Relationships: In a balanced delta connection, line voltages equal phase voltages: $$V_L = V_{ph}$$ Line current is the difference between two phase currents ($\mathbf{I}_R = \mathbf{I}_{RY} - \mathbf{I}_{BR}$): $$I_L = \sqrt{3} I_{ph} \quad (\text{Line current lags phase current by } 30^\circ)$$

In both star and delta balanced systems, total 3-phase active power is identically: $$P = 3 V_{ph} I_{ph} \cos\phi = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi \quad \text{Watts}$$ Total reactive power: $Q = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi \quad \text{VAR}$. Total apparent power: $S = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \quad \text{VA}$.

3. Two-Wattmeter Method for Power Measurement: Blondel's Theorem states that the power supplied to an $N$-wire system can be measured using $(N-1)$ wattmeters. For a 3-phase, 3-wire system (whether star or delta, balanced or unbalanced, with or without neutral), exactly $3 - 1 = 2$ wattmeters are sufficient.

Circuit Connection: - Current Coil of Wattmeter 1 ($W_1$) is inserted in line $R$; its Pressure (Voltage) Coil is connected between line $R$ and common line $Y$. - Current Coil of Wattmeter 2 ($W_2$) is inserted in line $B$; its Pressure Coil is connected between line $B$ and common line $Y$. - Line $Y$ acts as the common voltage reference point.

Mathematical Derivation for Balanced Star Load: Let the balanced load have an impedance of $\mathbf{Z} = Z \angle \phi$ per phase (lagging power factor angle $\phi$). Phase voltages with reference to neutral $N$: $$\mathbf{V}_{RN} = V_{ph} \angle 0^\circ, \quad \mathbf{V}_{YN} = V_{ph} \angle -120^\circ, \quad \mathbf{V}_{BN} = V_{ph} \angle -240^\circ = V_{ph} \angle +120^\circ$$ Phase and line currents (lagging respective phase voltages by $\phi$): $$\mathbf{I}_R = I_{ph} \angle -\phi, \quad \mathbf{I}_Y = I_{ph} \angle (-120^\circ - \phi), \quad \mathbf{I}_B = I_{ph} \angle (+120^\circ - \phi)$$

Wattmeter 1 Reading ($W_1$): Wattmeter 1 responds to the current through its current coil ($\mathbf{I}_R$) and the voltage across its pressure coil ($\mathbf{V}_{RY}$): $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = \mathbf{V}_{RN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \angle 30^\circ = V_L \angle 30^\circ$$ The phase angle between $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_R$ is: $$\theta_1 = \angle \mathbf{V}_{RY} - \angle \mathbf{I}_R = 30^\circ - (-\phi) = 30^\circ + \phi \quad \text{(or } 30^\circ - \phi \text{ depending on convention)}$$ By standard phasor geometry, $\mathbf{I}_R$ lags $\mathbf{V}_{RN}$ by $\phi$, while $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ leads $\mathbf{V}_{RN}$ by $30^\circ$. Hence, the angle between $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_R$ is $(30^\circ - \phi)$. $$W_1 = |\mathbf{V}_{RY}| |\mathbf{I}_R| \cos(30^\circ - \phi) = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ - \phi)$$

Wattmeter 2 Reading ($W_2$): Wattmeter 2 responds to current $\mathbf{I}_B$ and voltage $\mathbf{V}_{BY}$: $$\mathbf{V}_{BY} = \mathbf{V}_{BN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \angle 90^\circ = V_L \angle 90^\circ$$ Current $\mathbf{I}_B = I_L \angle (120^\circ - \phi)$. The phase angle between $\mathbf{V}_{BY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_B$ is: $$\theta_2 = (120^\circ - \phi) - 90^\circ = 30^\circ - \phi \quad \implies \angle(\mathbf{V}_{BY}, \mathbf{I}_B) = 30^\circ + \phi$$ $$W_2 = |\mathbf{V}_{BY}| |\mathbf{I}_B| \cos(30^\circ + \phi) = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ + \phi)$$

Sum of Wattmeter Readings: $$W_1 + W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ \cos(30^\circ - \phi) + \cos(30^\circ + \phi) \right]$$ Using trigonometric identity $\cos(A - B) + \cos(A + B) = 2 \cos A \cos B$: $$W_1 + W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cos 30^\circ \cos\phi \right] = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cdot \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cos\phi \right] = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi = P_{\text{total}}$$ Thus, the algebraic sum of the two wattmeter readings directly measures the total 3-phase active power under all conditions.

Difference of Wattmeter Readings: $$W_1 - W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ \cos(30^\circ - \phi) - \cos(30^\circ + \phi) \right]$$ Using $\cos(A - B) - \cos(A + B) = 2 \sin A \sin B$: $$W_1 - W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \sin 30^\circ \sin\phi \right] = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cdot \frac{1}{2} \sin\phi \right] = V_L I_L \sin\phi$$ Multiplying by $\sqrt{3}$: $$\sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2) = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi = Q_{\text{total}} \quad \text{(Total Reactive Power)}$$

Determination of Load Power Factor: Dividing the difference equation by the sum equation: $$\frac{\sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2)}{W_1 + W_2} = \frac{\sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi}{\sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi} = \tan\phi$$ $$\phi = \tan^{-1}\left( \sqrt{3} \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right)$$ $$\text{Power Factor } = \cos\phi = \cos\left( \tan^{-1}\left( \sqrt{3} \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right) \right) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + 3 \left( \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right)^2}}$$

Effect of Power Factor on Wattmeter Readings: 1. Unity Power Factor ($\cos\phi = 1, \phi = 0^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos 30^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$; $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos 30^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$. Both wattmeters read positive and equal values ($W_1 = W_2$). 2. Power Factor $0.5$ Lagging ($\phi = 60^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ - 60^\circ) = V_L I_L \cos(-30^\circ) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$. $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ + 60^\circ) = V_L I_L \cos 90^\circ = 0$. One wattmeter reads total power while the second wattmeter reads zero. 3. Power Factor $< 0.5$ Lagging ($60^\circ < \phi \le 90^\circ$): $W_1 > 0$, while $\cos(30^\circ + \phi) < 0 \implies W_2 < 0$. Wattmeter 2 pointer deflects backwards (downscale). To read $W_2$, the pressure coil or current coil connections must be reversed, and the reading recorded as a negative quantity: $P = W_1 - W_2$. 4. Zero Power Factor Purely Inductive ($\phi = 90^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos(-60^\circ) = \frac{1}{2} V_L I_L$; $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos(120^\circ) = -\frac{1}{2} V_L I_L$. $W_1 = -W_2$, so total active power $W_1 + W_2 = 0$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Polyphase systems, predominantly balanced three-phase AC systems, provide constant instantaneous power delivery, higher generator utilization, and reduced copper volume in transmission lines compared to single-phase systems.

1. Star (Y) Connection Relationships: In a balanced star connection, the three line terminals $R, Y, B$ are fed from phase windings meeting at a common neutral point $N$. - Line currents equal phase currents: $$I_L = I_{ph}$$ - The line voltage is the phasor difference between two phase voltages: $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = \mathbf{V}_{RN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN}$$ With balanced phase voltages $\mathbf{V}_{RN} = V_{ph} \angle 0^\circ$, $\mathbf{V}_{YN} = V_{ph} \angle -120^\circ$, $\mathbf{V}_{BN} = V_{ph} \angle -240^\circ$: $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = V_{ph} (1 - \cos(-120^\circ) - j\sin(-120^\circ)) = V_{ph}\left( 1 - \left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) + j\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right) = V_{ph}\left( \frac{3}{2} + j\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right)$$ $$|\mathbf{V}_{RY}| = V_{ph} \sqrt{\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)^2} = V_{ph} \sqrt{\frac{9}{4} + \frac{3}{4}} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph}$$ The phase angle is $\tan^{-1}\left( \frac{\sqrt{3}/2}{3/2} \right) = 30^\circ$. Thus: $$V_L = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \quad (\text{Line voltage leads phase voltage by } 30^\circ)$$

2. Delta ($\Delta$) Connection Relationships: In a balanced delta connection, line voltages equal phase voltages: $$V_L = V_{ph}$$ Line current is the difference between two phase currents ($\mathbf{I}_R = \mathbf{I}_{RY} - \mathbf{I}_{BR}$): $$I_L = \sqrt{3} I_{ph} \quad (\text{Line current lags phase current by } 30^\circ)$$

In both star and delta balanced systems, total 3-phase active power is identically: $$P = 3 V_{ph} I_{ph} \cos\phi = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi \quad \text{Watts}$$ Total reactive power: $Q = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi \quad \text{VAR}$. Total apparent power: $S = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \quad \text{VA}$.

3. Two-Wattmeter Method for Power Measurement: Blondel's Theorem states that the power supplied to an $N$-wire system can be measured using $(N-1)$ wattmeters. For a 3-phase, 3-wire system (whether star or delta, balanced or unbalanced, with or without neutral), exactly $3 - 1 = 2$ wattmeters are sufficient.

Circuit Connection: - Current Coil of Wattmeter 1 ($W_1$) is inserted in line $R$; its Pressure (Voltage) Coil is connected between line $R$ and common line $Y$. - Current Coil of Wattmeter 2 ($W_2$) is inserted in line $B$; its Pressure Coil is connected between line $B$ and common line $Y$. - Line $Y$ acts as the common voltage reference point.

Mathematical Derivation for Balanced Star Load: Let the balanced load have an impedance of $\mathbf{Z} = Z \angle \phi$ per phase (lagging power factor angle $\phi$). Phase voltages with reference to neutral $N$: $$\mathbf{V}_{RN} = V_{ph} \angle 0^\circ, \quad \mathbf{V}_{YN} = V_{ph} \angle -120^\circ, \quad \mathbf{V}_{BN} = V_{ph} \angle -240^\circ = V_{ph} \angle +120^\circ$$ Phase and line currents (lagging respective phase voltages by $\phi$): $$\mathbf{I}_R = I_{ph} \angle -\phi, \quad \mathbf{I}_Y = I_{ph} \angle (-120^\circ - \phi), \quad \mathbf{I}_B = I_{ph} \angle (+120^\circ - \phi)$$

Wattmeter 1 Reading ($W_1$): Wattmeter 1 responds to the current through its current coil ($\mathbf{I}_R$) and the voltage across its pressure coil ($\mathbf{V}_{RY}$): $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = \mathbf{V}_{RN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \angle 30^\circ = V_L \angle 30^\circ$$ The phase angle between $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_R$ is: $$\theta_1 = \angle \mathbf{V}_{RY} - \angle \mathbf{I}_R = 30^\circ - (-\phi) = 30^\circ + \phi \quad \text{(or } 30^\circ - \phi \text{ depending on convention)}$$ By standard phasor geometry, $\mathbf{I}_R$ lags $\mathbf{V}_{RN}$ by $\phi$, while $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ leads $\mathbf{V}_{RN}$ by $30^\circ$. Hence, the angle between $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_R$ is $(30^\circ - \phi)$. $$W_1 = |\mathbf{V}_{RY}| |\mathbf{I}_R| \cos(30^\circ - \phi) = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ - \phi)$$

Wattmeter 2 Reading ($W_2$): Wattmeter 2 responds to current $\mathbf{I}_B$ and voltage $\mathbf{V}_{BY}$: $$\mathbf{V}_{BY} = \mathbf{V}_{BN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \angle 90^\circ = V_L \angle 90^\circ$$ Current $\mathbf{I}_B = I_L \angle (120^\circ - \phi)$. The phase angle between $\mathbf{V}_{BY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_B$ is: $$\theta_2 = (120^\circ - \phi) - 90^\circ = 30^\circ - \phi \quad \implies \angle(\mathbf{V}_{BY}, \mathbf{I}_B) = 30^\circ + \phi$$ $$W_2 = |\mathbf{V}_{BY}| |\mathbf{I}_B| \cos(30^\circ + \phi) = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ + \phi)$$

Sum of Wattmeter Readings: $$W_1 + W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ \cos(30^\circ - \phi) + \cos(30^\circ + \phi) \right]$$ Using trigonometric identity $\cos(A - B) + \cos(A + B) = 2 \cos A \cos B$: $$W_1 + W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cos 30^\circ \cos\phi \right] = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cdot \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cos\phi \right] = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi = P_{\text{total}}$$ Thus, the algebraic sum of the two wattmeter readings directly measures the total 3-phase active power under all conditions.

Difference of Wattmeter Readings: $$W_1 - W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ \cos(30^\circ - \phi) - \cos(30^\circ + \phi) \right]$$ Using $\cos(A - B) - \cos(A + B) = 2 \sin A \sin B$: $$W_1 - W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \sin 30^\circ \sin\phi \right] = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cdot \frac{1}{2} \sin\phi \right] = V_L I_L \sin\phi$$ Multiplying by $\sqrt{3}$: $$\sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2) = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi = Q_{\text{total}} \quad \text{(Total Reactive Power)}$$

Determination of Load Power Factor: Dividing the difference equation by the sum equation: $$\frac{\sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2)}{W_1 + W_2} = \frac{\sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi}{\sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi} = \tan\phi$$ $$\phi = \tan^{-1}\left( \sqrt{3} \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right)$$ $$\text{Power Factor } = \cos\phi = \cos\left( \tan^{-1}\left( \sqrt{3} \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right) \right) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + 3 \left( \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right)^2}}$$

Effect of Power Factor on Wattmeter Readings: 1. Unity Power Factor ($\cos\phi = 1, \phi = 0^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos 30^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$; $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos 30^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$. Both wattmeters read positive and equal values ($W_1 = W_2$). 2. Power Factor $0.5$ Lagging ($\phi = 60^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ - 60^\circ) = V_L I_L \cos(-30^\circ) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$. $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ + 60^\circ) = V_L I_L \cos 90^\circ = 0$. One wattmeter reads total power while the second wattmeter reads zero. 3. Power Factor $< 0.5$ Lagging ($60^\circ < \phi \le 90^\circ$): $W_1 > 0$, while $\cos(30^\circ + \phi) < 0 \implies W_2 < 0$. Wattmeter 2 pointer deflects backwards (downscale). To read $W_2$, the pressure coil or current coil connections must be reversed, and the reading recorded as a negative quantity: $P = W_1 - W_2$. 4. Zero Power Factor Purely Inductive ($\phi = 90^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos(-60^\circ) = \frac{1}{2} V_L I_L$; $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos(120^\circ) = -\frac{1}{2} V_L I_L$. $W_1 = -W_2$, so total active power $W_1 + W_2 = 0$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Polyphase systems, predominantly balanced three-phase AC systems, provide constant instantaneous power delivery, higher generator utilization, and reduced copper volume in transmission lines compared to single-phase systems.

1. Star (Y) Connection Relationships: In a balanced star connection, the three line terminals $R, Y, B$ are fed from phase windings meeting at a common neutral point $N$. - Line currents equal phase currents: $$I_L = I_{ph}$$ - The line voltage is the phasor difference between two phase voltages: $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = \mathbf{V}_{RN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN}$$ With balanced phase voltages $\mathbf{V}_{RN} = V_{ph} \angle 0^\circ$, $\mathbf{V}_{YN} = V_{ph} \angle -120^\circ$, $\mathbf{V}_{BN} = V_{ph} \angle -240^\circ$: $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = V_{ph} (1 - \cos(-120^\circ) - j\sin(-120^\circ)) = V_{ph}\left( 1 - \left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) + j\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right) = V_{ph}\left( \frac{3}{2} + j\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \right)$$ $$|\mathbf{V}_{RY}| = V_{ph} \sqrt{\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)^2} = V_{ph} \sqrt{\frac{9}{4} + \frac{3}{4}} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph}$$ The phase angle is $\tan^{-1}\left( \frac{\sqrt{3}/2}{3/2} \right) = 30^\circ$. Thus: $$V_L = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \quad (\text{Line voltage leads phase voltage by } 30^\circ)$$

2. Delta ($\Delta$) Connection Relationships: In a balanced delta connection, line voltages equal phase voltages: $$V_L = V_{ph}$$ Line current is the difference between two phase currents ($\mathbf{I}_R = \mathbf{I}_{RY} - \mathbf{I}_{BR}$): $$I_L = \sqrt{3} I_{ph} \quad (\text{Line current lags phase current by } 30^\circ)$$

In both star and delta balanced systems, total 3-phase active power is identically: $$P = 3 V_{ph} I_{ph} \cos\phi = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi \quad \text{Watts}$$ Total reactive power: $Q = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi \quad \text{VAR}$. Total apparent power: $S = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \quad \text{VA}$.

3. Two-Wattmeter Method for Power Measurement: Blondel's Theorem states that the power supplied to an $N$-wire system can be measured using $(N-1)$ wattmeters. For a 3-phase, 3-wire system (whether star or delta, balanced or unbalanced, with or without neutral), exactly $3 - 1 = 2$ wattmeters are sufficient.

Circuit Connection: - Current Coil of Wattmeter 1 ($W_1$) is inserted in line $R$; its Pressure (Voltage) Coil is connected between line $R$ and common line $Y$. - Current Coil of Wattmeter 2 ($W_2$) is inserted in line $B$; its Pressure Coil is connected between line $B$ and common line $Y$. - Line $Y$ acts as the common voltage reference point.

Mathematical Derivation for Balanced Star Load: Let the balanced load have an impedance of $\mathbf{Z} = Z \angle \phi$ per phase (lagging power factor angle $\phi$). Phase voltages with reference to neutral $N$: $$\mathbf{V}_{RN} = V_{ph} \angle 0^\circ, \quad \mathbf{V}_{YN} = V_{ph} \angle -120^\circ, \quad \mathbf{V}_{BN} = V_{ph} \angle -240^\circ = V_{ph} \angle +120^\circ$$ Phase and line currents (lagging respective phase voltages by $\phi$): $$\mathbf{I}_R = I_{ph} \angle -\phi, \quad \mathbf{I}_Y = I_{ph} \angle (-120^\circ - \phi), \quad \mathbf{I}_B = I_{ph} \angle (+120^\circ - \phi)$$

Wattmeter 1 Reading ($W_1$): Wattmeter 1 responds to the current through its current coil ($\mathbf{I}_R$) and the voltage across its pressure coil ($\mathbf{V}_{RY}$): $$\mathbf{V}_{RY} = \mathbf{V}_{RN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \angle 30^\circ = V_L \angle 30^\circ$$ The phase angle between $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_R$ is: $$\theta_1 = \angle \mathbf{V}_{RY} - \angle \mathbf{I}_R = 30^\circ - (-\phi) = 30^\circ + \phi \quad \text{(or } 30^\circ - \phi \text{ depending on convention)}$$ By standard phasor geometry, $\mathbf{I}_R$ lags $\mathbf{V}_{RN}$ by $\phi$, while $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ leads $\mathbf{V}_{RN}$ by $30^\circ$. Hence, the angle between $\mathbf{V}_{RY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_R$ is $(30^\circ - \phi)$. $$W_1 = |\mathbf{V}_{RY}| |\mathbf{I}_R| \cos(30^\circ - \phi) = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ - \phi)$$

Wattmeter 2 Reading ($W_2$): Wattmeter 2 responds to current $\mathbf{I}_B$ and voltage $\mathbf{V}_{BY}$: $$\mathbf{V}_{BY} = \mathbf{V}_{BN} - \mathbf{V}_{YN} = \sqrt{3} V_{ph} \angle 90^\circ = V_L \angle 90^\circ$$ Current $\mathbf{I}_B = I_L \angle (120^\circ - \phi)$. The phase angle between $\mathbf{V}_{BY}$ and $\mathbf{I}_B$ is: $$\theta_2 = (120^\circ - \phi) - 90^\circ = 30^\circ - \phi \quad \implies \angle(\mathbf{V}_{BY}, \mathbf{I}_B) = 30^\circ + \phi$$ $$W_2 = |\mathbf{V}_{BY}| |\mathbf{I}_B| \cos(30^\circ + \phi) = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ + \phi)$$

Sum of Wattmeter Readings: $$W_1 + W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ \cos(30^\circ - \phi) + \cos(30^\circ + \phi) \right]$$ Using trigonometric identity $\cos(A - B) + \cos(A + B) = 2 \cos A \cos B$: $$W_1 + W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cos 30^\circ \cos\phi \right] = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cdot \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cos\phi \right] = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi = P_{\text{total}}$$ Thus, the algebraic sum of the two wattmeter readings directly measures the total 3-phase active power under all conditions.

Difference of Wattmeter Readings: $$W_1 - W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ \cos(30^\circ - \phi) - \cos(30^\circ + \phi) \right]$$ Using $\cos(A - B) - \cos(A + B) = 2 \sin A \sin B$: $$W_1 - W_2 = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \sin 30^\circ \sin\phi \right] = V_L I_L \left[ 2 \cdot \frac{1}{2} \sin\phi \right] = V_L I_L \sin\phi$$ Multiplying by $\sqrt{3}$: $$\sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2) = \sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi = Q_{\text{total}} \quad \text{(Total Reactive Power)}$$

Determination of Load Power Factor: Dividing the difference equation by the sum equation: $$\frac{\sqrt{3}(W_1 - W_2)}{W_1 + W_2} = \frac{\sqrt{3} V_L I_L \sin\phi}{\sqrt{3} V_L I_L \cos\phi} = \tan\phi$$ $$\phi = \tan^{-1}\left( \sqrt{3} \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right)$$ $$\text{Power Factor } = \cos\phi = \cos\left( \tan^{-1}\left( \sqrt{3} \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right) \right) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + 3 \left( \frac{W_1 - W_2}{W_1 + W_2} \right)^2}}$$

Effect of Power Factor on Wattmeter Readings: 1. Unity Power Factor ($\cos\phi = 1, \phi = 0^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos 30^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$; $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos 30^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$. Both wattmeters read positive and equal values ($W_1 = W_2$). 2. Power Factor $0.5$ Lagging ($\phi = 60^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ - 60^\circ) = V_L I_L \cos(-30^\circ) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} V_L I_L$. $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos(30^\circ + 60^\circ) = V_L I_L \cos 90^\circ = 0$. One wattmeter reads total power while the second wattmeter reads zero. 3. Power Factor $< 0.5$ Lagging ($60^\circ < \phi \le 90^\circ$): $W_1 > 0$, while $\cos(30^\circ + \phi) < 0 \implies W_2 < 0$. Wattmeter 2 pointer deflects backwards (downscale). To read $W_2$, the pressure coil or current coil connections must be reversed, and the reading recorded as a negative quantity: $P = W_1 - W_2$. 4. Zero Power Factor Purely Inductive ($\phi = 90^\circ$): $W_1 = V_L I_L \cos(-60^\circ) = \frac{1}{2} V_L I_L$; $W_2 = V_L I_L \cos(120^\circ) = -\frac{1}{2} V_L I_L$. $W_1 = -W_2$, so total active power $W_1 + W_2 = 0$.

Q5. State and explain Maxwell's four equations in differential and integral forms. Derive the electromagnetic wave equation in a lossless medium and state Poynting's theorem. Q5. State and explain Maxwell's four equations in differential and integral forms. Derive the electromagnetic wave equation in a lossless medium and state Poynting's theorem. Q5. State and explain Maxwell's four equations in differential and integral forms. Derive the electromagnetic wave equation in a lossless medium and state Poynting's theorem.
