Induction Motors: Balanced 3-phase stator currents produce an RMF of constant magnitude $1.5\Phi_m$ revolving at $N_s$. Breakdown torque $T_{max}$ is independent of rotor resistance $R_2$. Star-delta starting reduces starting line current and torque to $1/3$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The three-phase induction motor, often called the workhorse of industrial automation, is a singly excited asynchronous machine wherein rotor currents are established entirely via electromagnetic induction from the stator rotating magnetic field.

1. Proof of Rotating Magnetic Field (RMF): Consider a symmetrical three-phase stator winding with phase coils spatially displaced from each other by $120^\circ$ electrical around the stator bore. Energizing these windings with balanced sinusoidal three-phase currents: $$i_R(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t)$$ $$i_Y(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)$$ $$i_B(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t - 240^\circ) = I_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)$$ Each phase current establishes an alternating spatial flux along its respective magnetic axis: $$\Phi_R = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t)$$ $$\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)$$ $$\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)$$

Evaluating the resultant flux $\mathbf{\Phi}_r$ at different electrical time instants: - Instant 1: $\omega t = 0$: $\Phi_R = 0$ $\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(-120^\circ) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(+120^\circ) = +\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ Resolving along horizontal and vertical axes: $$\Phi_{rx} = \Phi_R - \Phi_Y \cos 60^\circ - \Phi_B \cos 60^\circ = 0 - \left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{1}{2} - \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{1}{2} = 0$$ $$\Phi_{ry} = 0 - \Phi_Y \sin 60^\circ + \Phi_B \sin 60^\circ = -\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{3}{4}\Phi_m + \frac{3}{4}\Phi_m = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m$$ Resultant magnitude: $$\Phi_r = \sqrt{\Phi_{rx}^2 + \Phi_{ry}^2} = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m = 1.5 \Phi_m \quad \text{directed at } 90^\circ$$

- Instant 2: $\omega t = 60^\circ$: $\Phi_R = \Phi_m \sin 60^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(-60^\circ) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(180^\circ) = 0$ Computing the resultant: $$\Phi_r = 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m \right) \cos 30^\circ = 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m \right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m = 1.5 \Phi_m \quad \text{rotated by } 60^\circ$$

General Mathematical Proof: Using trigonometric identities for any arbitrary $\omega t$: $$\Phi_r(\theta, t) = \Phi_m \sin\omega t \cos\theta + \Phi_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)\cos(\theta - 120^\circ) + \Phi_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)\cos(\theta + 120^\circ)$$ Using the product-to-sum identity $\sin A \cos B = \frac{1}{2}[\sin(A - B) + \sin(A + B)]$: $$\Phi_r(\theta, t) = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m \sin(\omega t - \theta)$$ This represents a traveling magnetic wave of constant magnitude $1.5 \Phi_m$ revolving uniformly around the air-gap at Synchronous Speed: $$N_s = \frac{120 f}{P} \quad \text{rpm} \quad \text{or} \quad \omega_s = \frac{4\pi f}{P} \quad \text{rad/s}$$

2. Slip and Rotor Electrical Parameters: Slip $s$ is the fractional difference between synchronous speed and rotor speed $N$: $$s = \frac{N_s - N}{N_s} \implies N = N_s (1 - s)$$ - Rotor induced frequency: $f_r = s f$ - Rotor induced EMF at slip $s$: $E_{2s} = s E_2$ - Rotor standstill leakage reactance: $X_2 = 2\pi f L_2$ - Rotor running leakage reactance: $X_{2s} = 2\pi f_r L_2 = s X_2$ - Rotor current per phase: $$I_2 = \frac{E_{2s}}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + X_{2s}^2}} = \frac{s E_2}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2}} = \frac{E_2}{\sqrt{(R_2/s)^2 + X_2^2}}$$ - Rotor power factor: $\cos\phi_2 = \frac{R_2}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2}} = \frac{R_2/s}{\sqrt{(R_2/s)^2 + X_2^2}}$

3. Derivation of Electromagnetic Torque Equation: The total mechanical air-gap power transferred across the stator-rotor boundary for a 3-phase motor is: $$P_g = 3 I_2^2 \left(\frac{R_2}{s}\right) = 3 \left( \frac{s^2 E_2^2}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2} \right) \frac{R_2}{s} = \frac{3 s E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2}$$ Rotor copper loss: $P_{cu,rotor} = 3 I_2^2 R_2 = s P_g$. Gross mechanical power developed: $P_{mech} = P_g - P_{cu,rotor} = (1 - s) P_g$. Electromagnetic torque $T_e$ is air-gap power divided by synchronous mechanical speed: $$T_e = \frac{P_{mech}}{\omega_m} = \frac{(1 - s) P_g}{(1 - s)\omega_s} = \frac{P_g}{\omega_s}$$ $$T_e = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \cdot \frac{s E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2} \quad \text{N}\cdot\text{m}$$

Analysis of Torque-Slip Characteristics: - Low-Slip Region (Normal Operation, $s \ll 1$): Here $(s X_2)^2 \ll R_2^2$, so the reactance term is negligible: $$T_e \approx \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{s E_2^2}{R_2} \implies T_e \propto s$$ Torque varies linearly with slip; the motor operates stably with high efficiency. - High-Slip Region ($s \to 1$, Starting Condition): Here $(s X_2)^2 \gg R_2^2$, so resistance is negligible: $$T_e \approx \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{s E_2^2 R_2}{s^2 X_2^2} = \frac{3 E_2^2 R_2}{\omega_s X_2^2} \cdot \frac{1}{s} \implies T_e \propto \frac{1}{s}$$ Torque is inversely proportional to slip (unstable region).

Condition for Maximum (Breakdown) Torque ($T_{max}$): Differentiating $T_e$ with respect to slip $s$ and setting $\frac{dT_e}{ds} = 0$: $$\frac{d}{ds}\left[ \frac{s}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2} \right] = \frac{(R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2)\cdot 1 - s(2s X_2^2)}{(R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2)^2} = 0$$ $$R_2^2 - s^2 X_2^2 = 0 \implies s_{\max} = \frac{R_2}{X_2}$$ Maximum torque occurs at a slip equal to the ratio of rotor resistance to standstill reactance. Substituting $s_{\max} = R_2 / X_2$ into the torque equation: $$T_{\max} = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{(R_2 / X_2) E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + (R_2 / X_2)^2 X_2^2} = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{(R_2^2 / X_2) E_2^2}{2 R_2^2}$$ $$T_{\max} = \frac{3}{2 \omega_s} \frac{E_2^2}{X_2}$$ Key Insight: Maximum breakdown torque is completely independent of rotor resistance $R_2$. Adding external resistance to a wound-rotor induction motor shifts the point of maximum torque toward standstill ($s = 1$), enabling maximum torque at starting ($s_{max} = 1$ when $R_2 = X_2$) without diminishing $T_{max}$.

4. Starting Methods of Three-Phase Induction Motors: Direct-On-Line (DOL) starting draws $5-7$ times rated full-load current ($I_{st} = 5 \text{ to } 7 I_{fl}$), causing unacceptable network voltage sags. - Star-Delta ($\text{Y}-\Delta$) Starter: Windings are connected in Star during starting, then switched to Delta after acceleration ($> 80\% N_s$). - Starting phase voltage: $V_{ph,Y} = V_L / \sqrt{3}$ - Starting line current: $I_{st,Y} = \frac{V_L / \sqrt{3}}{Z_{st}} = \frac{1}{3} \left( \frac{\sqrt{3} V_L}{Z_{st}} \right) = \frac{1}{3} I_{st,\Delta}$ - Starting torque: $T_{st,Y} = \frac{1}{3} T_{st,\Delta}$ Both starting current and starting torque are reduced to exactly one-third ($33.3\%$) of their DOL values. - Auto-Transformer Starter: Tappings on an autotransformer supply a fraction $x$ ($x = 0.5, 0.65, 0.8$) of line voltage to the stator: - Motor terminal voltage: $V_{st} = x V_L$ - Motor starting current: $I_{motor} = x I_{sc}$ - Line starting current drawn from mains: $I_{line} = x \cdot I_{motor} = x^2 I_{sc}$ - Starting torque: $T_{st} = x^2 T_{sc} = x^2 \left( \frac{I_{sc}}{I_{fl}} \right)^2 s_{fl} T_{fl}$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The three-phase induction motor, often called the workhorse of industrial automation, is a singly excited asynchronous machine wherein rotor currents are established entirely via electromagnetic induction from the stator rotating magnetic field.

1. Proof of Rotating Magnetic Field (RMF): Consider a symmetrical three-phase stator winding with phase coils spatially displaced from each other by $120^\circ$ electrical around the stator bore. Energizing these windings with balanced sinusoidal three-phase currents: $$i_R(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t)$$ $$i_Y(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)$$ $$i_B(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t - 240^\circ) = I_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)$$ Each phase current establishes an alternating spatial flux along its respective magnetic axis: $$\Phi_R = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t)$$ $$\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)$$ $$\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)$$

Evaluating the resultant flux $\mathbf{\Phi}_r$ at different electrical time instants: - Instant 1: $\omega t = 0$: $\Phi_R = 0$ $\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(-120^\circ) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(+120^\circ) = +\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ Resolving along horizontal and vertical axes: $$\Phi_{rx} = \Phi_R - \Phi_Y \cos 60^\circ - \Phi_B \cos 60^\circ = 0 - \left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{1}{2} - \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{1}{2} = 0$$ $$\Phi_{ry} = 0 - \Phi_Y \sin 60^\circ + \Phi_B \sin 60^\circ = -\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{3}{4}\Phi_m + \frac{3}{4}\Phi_m = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m$$ Resultant magnitude: $$\Phi_r = \sqrt{\Phi_{rx}^2 + \Phi_{ry}^2} = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m = 1.5 \Phi_m \quad \text{directed at } 90^\circ$$

- Instant 2: $\omega t = 60^\circ$: $\Phi_R = \Phi_m \sin 60^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(-60^\circ) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(180^\circ) = 0$ Computing the resultant: $$\Phi_r = 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m \right) \cos 30^\circ = 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m \right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m = 1.5 \Phi_m \quad \text{rotated by } 60^\circ$$

General Mathematical Proof: Using trigonometric identities for any arbitrary $\omega t$: $$\Phi_r(\theta, t) = \Phi_m \sin\omega t \cos\theta + \Phi_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)\cos(\theta - 120^\circ) + \Phi_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)\cos(\theta + 120^\circ)$$ Using the product-to-sum identity $\sin A \cos B = \frac{1}{2}[\sin(A - B) + \sin(A + B)]$: $$\Phi_r(\theta, t) = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m \sin(\omega t - \theta)$$ This represents a traveling magnetic wave of constant magnitude $1.5 \Phi_m$ revolving uniformly around the air-gap at Synchronous Speed: $$N_s = \frac{120 f}{P} \quad \text{rpm} \quad \text{or} \quad \omega_s = \frac{4\pi f}{P} \quad \text{rad/s}$$

2. Slip and Rotor Electrical Parameters: Slip $s$ is the fractional difference between synchronous speed and rotor speed $N$: $$s = \frac{N_s - N}{N_s} \implies N = N_s (1 - s)$$ - Rotor induced frequency: $f_r = s f$ - Rotor induced EMF at slip $s$: $E_{2s} = s E_2$ - Rotor standstill leakage reactance: $X_2 = 2\pi f L_2$ - Rotor running leakage reactance: $X_{2s} = 2\pi f_r L_2 = s X_2$ - Rotor current per phase: $$I_2 = \frac{E_{2s}}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + X_{2s}^2}} = \frac{s E_2}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2}} = \frac{E_2}{\sqrt{(R_2/s)^2 + X_2^2}}$$ - Rotor power factor: $\cos\phi_2 = \frac{R_2}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2}} = \frac{R_2/s}{\sqrt{(R_2/s)^2 + X_2^2}}$

3. Derivation of Electromagnetic Torque Equation: The total mechanical air-gap power transferred across the stator-rotor boundary for a 3-phase motor is: $$P_g = 3 I_2^2 \left(\frac{R_2}{s}\right) = 3 \left( \frac{s^2 E_2^2}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2} \right) \frac{R_2}{s} = \frac{3 s E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2}$$ Rotor copper loss: $P_{cu,rotor} = 3 I_2^2 R_2 = s P_g$. Gross mechanical power developed: $P_{mech} = P_g - P_{cu,rotor} = (1 - s) P_g$. Electromagnetic torque $T_e$ is air-gap power divided by synchronous mechanical speed: $$T_e = \frac{P_{mech}}{\omega_m} = \frac{(1 - s) P_g}{(1 - s)\omega_s} = \frac{P_g}{\omega_s}$$ $$T_e = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \cdot \frac{s E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2} \quad \text{N}\cdot\text{m}$$

Analysis of Torque-Slip Characteristics: - Low-Slip Region (Normal Operation, $s \ll 1$): Here $(s X_2)^2 \ll R_2^2$, so the reactance term is negligible: $$T_e \approx \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{s E_2^2}{R_2} \implies T_e \propto s$$ Torque varies linearly with slip; the motor operates stably with high efficiency. - High-Slip Region ($s \to 1$, Starting Condition): Here $(s X_2)^2 \gg R_2^2$, so resistance is negligible: $$T_e \approx \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{s E_2^2 R_2}{s^2 X_2^2} = \frac{3 E_2^2 R_2}{\omega_s X_2^2} \cdot \frac{1}{s} \implies T_e \propto \frac{1}{s}$$ Torque is inversely proportional to slip (unstable region).

Condition for Maximum (Breakdown) Torque ($T_{max}$): Differentiating $T_e$ with respect to slip $s$ and setting $\frac{dT_e}{ds} = 0$: $$\frac{d}{ds}\left[ \frac{s}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2} \right] = \frac{(R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2)\cdot 1 - s(2s X_2^2)}{(R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2)^2} = 0$$ $$R_2^2 - s^2 X_2^2 = 0 \implies s_{\max} = \frac{R_2}{X_2}$$ Maximum torque occurs at a slip equal to the ratio of rotor resistance to standstill reactance. Substituting $s_{\max} = R_2 / X_2$ into the torque equation: $$T_{\max} = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{(R_2 / X_2) E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + (R_2 / X_2)^2 X_2^2} = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{(R_2^2 / X_2) E_2^2}{2 R_2^2}$$ $$T_{\max} = \frac{3}{2 \omega_s} \frac{E_2^2}{X_2}$$ Key Insight: Maximum breakdown torque is completely independent of rotor resistance $R_2$. Adding external resistance to a wound-rotor induction motor shifts the point of maximum torque toward standstill ($s = 1$), enabling maximum torque at starting ($s_{max} = 1$ when $R_2 = X_2$) without diminishing $T_{max}$.

4. Starting Methods of Three-Phase Induction Motors: Direct-On-Line (DOL) starting draws $5-7$ times rated full-load current ($I_{st} = 5 \text{ to } 7 I_{fl}$), causing unacceptable network voltage sags. - Star-Delta ($\text{Y}-\Delta$) Starter: Windings are connected in Star during starting, then switched to Delta after acceleration ($> 80\% N_s$). - Starting phase voltage: $V_{ph,Y} = V_L / \sqrt{3}$ - Starting line current: $I_{st,Y} = \frac{V_L / \sqrt{3}}{Z_{st}} = \frac{1}{3} \left( \frac{\sqrt{3} V_L}{Z_{st}} \right) = \frac{1}{3} I_{st,\Delta}$ - Starting torque: $T_{st,Y} = \frac{1}{3} T_{st,\Delta}$ Both starting current and starting torque are reduced to exactly one-third ($33.3\%$) of their DOL values. - Auto-Transformer Starter: Tappings on an autotransformer supply a fraction $x$ ($x = 0.5, 0.65, 0.8$) of line voltage to the stator: - Motor terminal voltage: $V_{st} = x V_L$ - Motor starting current: $I_{motor} = x I_{sc}$ - Line starting current drawn from mains: $I_{line} = x \cdot I_{motor} = x^2 I_{sc}$ - Starting torque: $T_{st} = x^2 T_{sc} = x^2 \left( \frac{I_{sc}}{I_{fl}} \right)^2 s_{fl} T_{fl}$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The three-phase induction motor, often called the workhorse of industrial automation, is a singly excited asynchronous machine wherein rotor currents are established entirely via electromagnetic induction from the stator rotating magnetic field.

1. Proof of Rotating Magnetic Field (RMF): Consider a symmetrical three-phase stator winding with phase coils spatially displaced from each other by $120^\circ$ electrical around the stator bore. Energizing these windings with balanced sinusoidal three-phase currents: $$i_R(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t)$$ $$i_Y(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)$$ $$i_B(t) = I_m \sin(\omega t - 240^\circ) = I_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)$$ Each phase current establishes an alternating spatial flux along its respective magnetic axis: $$\Phi_R = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t)$$ $$\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)$$ $$\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)$$

Evaluating the resultant flux $\mathbf{\Phi}_r$ at different electrical time instants: - Instant 1: $\omega t = 0$: $\Phi_R = 0$ $\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(-120^\circ) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(+120^\circ) = +\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ Resolving along horizontal and vertical axes: $$\Phi_{rx} = \Phi_R - \Phi_Y \cos 60^\circ - \Phi_B \cos 60^\circ = 0 - \left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{1}{2} - \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{1}{2} = 0$$ $$\Phi_{ry} = 0 - \Phi_Y \sin 60^\circ + \Phi_B \sin 60^\circ = -\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m\right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{3}{4}\Phi_m + \frac{3}{4}\Phi_m = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m$$ Resultant magnitude: $$\Phi_r = \sqrt{\Phi_{rx}^2 + \Phi_{ry}^2} = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m = 1.5 \Phi_m \quad \text{directed at } 90^\circ$$

- Instant 2: $\omega t = 60^\circ$: $\Phi_R = \Phi_m \sin 60^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_Y = \Phi_m \sin(-60^\circ) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m$ $\Phi_B = \Phi_m \sin(180^\circ) = 0$ Computing the resultant: $$\Phi_r = 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m \right) \cos 30^\circ = 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Phi_m \right)\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m = 1.5 \Phi_m \quad \text{rotated by } 60^\circ$$

General Mathematical Proof: Using trigonometric identities for any arbitrary $\omega t$: $$\Phi_r(\theta, t) = \Phi_m \sin\omega t \cos\theta + \Phi_m \sin(\omega t - 120^\circ)\cos(\theta - 120^\circ) + \Phi_m \sin(\omega t + 120^\circ)\cos(\theta + 120^\circ)$$ Using the product-to-sum identity $\sin A \cos B = \frac{1}{2}[\sin(A - B) + \sin(A + B)]$: $$\Phi_r(\theta, t) = \frac{3}{2}\Phi_m \sin(\omega t - \theta)$$ This represents a traveling magnetic wave of constant magnitude $1.5 \Phi_m$ revolving uniformly around the air-gap at Synchronous Speed: $$N_s = \frac{120 f}{P} \quad \text{rpm} \quad \text{or} \quad \omega_s = \frac{4\pi f}{P} \quad \text{rad/s}$$

2. Slip and Rotor Electrical Parameters: Slip $s$ is the fractional difference between synchronous speed and rotor speed $N$: $$s = \frac{N_s - N}{N_s} \implies N = N_s (1 - s)$$ - Rotor induced frequency: $f_r = s f$ - Rotor induced EMF at slip $s$: $E_{2s} = s E_2$ - Rotor standstill leakage reactance: $X_2 = 2\pi f L_2$ - Rotor running leakage reactance: $X_{2s} = 2\pi f_r L_2 = s X_2$ - Rotor current per phase: $$I_2 = \frac{E_{2s}}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + X_{2s}^2}} = \frac{s E_2}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2}} = \frac{E_2}{\sqrt{(R_2/s)^2 + X_2^2}}$$ - Rotor power factor: $\cos\phi_2 = \frac{R_2}{\sqrt{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2}} = \frac{R_2/s}{\sqrt{(R_2/s)^2 + X_2^2}}$

3. Derivation of Electromagnetic Torque Equation: The total mechanical air-gap power transferred across the stator-rotor boundary for a 3-phase motor is: $$P_g = 3 I_2^2 \left(\frac{R_2}{s}\right) = 3 \left( \frac{s^2 E_2^2}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2} \right) \frac{R_2}{s} = \frac{3 s E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2}$$ Rotor copper loss: $P_{cu,rotor} = 3 I_2^2 R_2 = s P_g$. Gross mechanical power developed: $P_{mech} = P_g - P_{cu,rotor} = (1 - s) P_g$. Electromagnetic torque $T_e$ is air-gap power divided by synchronous mechanical speed: $$T_e = \frac{P_{mech}}{\omega_m} = \frac{(1 - s) P_g}{(1 - s)\omega_s} = \frac{P_g}{\omega_s}$$ $$T_e = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \cdot \frac{s E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + (s X_2)^2} \quad \text{N}\cdot\text{m}$$

Analysis of Torque-Slip Characteristics: - Low-Slip Region (Normal Operation, $s \ll 1$): Here $(s X_2)^2 \ll R_2^2$, so the reactance term is negligible: $$T_e \approx \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{s E_2^2}{R_2} \implies T_e \propto s$$ Torque varies linearly with slip; the motor operates stably with high efficiency. - High-Slip Region ($s \to 1$, Starting Condition): Here $(s X_2)^2 \gg R_2^2$, so resistance is negligible: $$T_e \approx \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{s E_2^2 R_2}{s^2 X_2^2} = \frac{3 E_2^2 R_2}{\omega_s X_2^2} \cdot \frac{1}{s} \implies T_e \propto \frac{1}{s}$$ Torque is inversely proportional to slip (unstable region).

Condition for Maximum (Breakdown) Torque ($T_{max}$): Differentiating $T_e$ with respect to slip $s$ and setting $\frac{dT_e}{ds} = 0$: $$\frac{d}{ds}\left[ \frac{s}{R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2} \right] = \frac{(R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2)\cdot 1 - s(2s X_2^2)}{(R_2^2 + s^2 X_2^2)^2} = 0$$ $$R_2^2 - s^2 X_2^2 = 0 \implies s_{\max} = \frac{R_2}{X_2}$$ Maximum torque occurs at a slip equal to the ratio of rotor resistance to standstill reactance. Substituting $s_{\max} = R_2 / X_2$ into the torque equation: $$T_{\max} = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{(R_2 / X_2) E_2^2 R_2}{R_2^2 + (R_2 / X_2)^2 X_2^2} = \frac{3}{\omega_s} \frac{(R_2^2 / X_2) E_2^2}{2 R_2^2}$$ $$T_{\max} = \frac{3}{2 \omega_s} \frac{E_2^2}{X_2}$$ Key Insight: Maximum breakdown torque is completely independent of rotor resistance $R_2$. Adding external resistance to a wound-rotor induction motor shifts the point of maximum torque toward standstill ($s = 1$), enabling maximum torque at starting ($s_{max} = 1$ when $R_2 = X_2$) without diminishing $T_{max}$.

4. Starting Methods of Three-Phase Induction Motors: Direct-On-Line (DOL) starting draws $5-7$ times rated full-load current ($I_{st} = 5 \text{ to } 7 I_{fl}$), causing unacceptable network voltage sags. - Star-Delta ($\text{Y}-\Delta$) Starter: Windings are connected in Star during starting, then switched to Delta after acceleration ($> 80\% N_s$). - Starting phase voltage: $V_{ph,Y} = V_L / \sqrt{3}$ - Starting line current: $I_{st,Y} = \frac{V_L / \sqrt{3}}{Z_{st}} = \frac{1}{3} \left( \frac{\sqrt{3} V_L}{Z_{st}} \right) = \frac{1}{3} I_{st,\Delta}$ - Starting torque: $T_{st,Y} = \frac{1}{3} T_{st,\Delta}$ Both starting current and starting torque are reduced to exactly one-third ($33.3\%$) of their DOL values. - Auto-Transformer Starter: Tappings on an autotransformer supply a fraction $x$ ($x = 0.5, 0.65, 0.8$) of line voltage to the stator: - Motor terminal voltage: $V_{st} = x V_L$ - Motor starting current: $I_{motor} = x I_{sc}$ - Line starting current drawn from mains: $I_{line} = x \cdot I_{motor} = x^2 I_{sc}$ - Starting torque: $T_{st} = x^2 T_{sc} = x^2 \left( \frac{I_{sc}}{I_{fl}} \right)^2 s_{fl} T_{fl}$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Synchronous machines are doubly excited AC machines operating strictly at synchronous speed $N_s = 120 f / P$. Their voltage regulation under changing load power factor is a critical stability parameter.

1. Generated EMF Equation & Winding Factors: For a 3-phase alternator with flux per pole $\Phi$ ($\text{Wb}$), frequency $f$ ($\text{Hz}$), and series turns per phase $T_{ph}$: $$E_{ph} = 4.44 k_p k_d f \Phi T_{ph} \quad \text{Volts}$$ - Pitch Factor (Chording Factor) $k_p$: Accounts for short-pitching coils by chording angle $\alpha$: $$k_p = \cos\left( \frac{\alpha}{2} \right)$$ Short pitching eliminates specific harmonic orders (e.g., setting $\alpha = 180^\circ / n$ eliminates the $n$-th harmonic) and reduces end-turn copper volume. - Distribution (Breadth) Factor $k_d$: Accounts for distributing coils over $m$ slots per pole per phase with slot pitch angle $\beta = 180^\circ / (\text{slots/pole})$: $$k_d = \frac{\sin(m\beta / 2)}{m \sin(\beta / 2)}$$

2. Voltage Regulation Determination: Voltage regulation is defined as: $$\%VR = \frac{|E_{0}| - |V|}{V} \times 100\%$$ where $E_0$ is the no-load open-circuit terminal voltage for the same excitation field current $I_f$ that yields rated terminal voltage $V$ at rated load.

Method A: Synchronous Impedance (EMF) Method: 1. Conduct Open-Circuit Test to obtain the Open-Circuit Characteristic (OCC: $V_{oc}$ vs $I_f$). 2. Conduct Short-Circuit Test to obtain the Short-Circuit Characteristic (SCC: $I_{sc}$ vs $I_f$). 3. For a specific field current $I_f$: $$Z_s = \left. \frac{V_{oc,ph}}{I_{sc,ph}} \right|_{I_f = \text{const}}$$ 4. Measure DC armature resistance and correct for skin effect: $R_a \approx 1.25 R_{dc}$. 5. Calculate Synchronous Reactance: $X_s = \sqrt{Z_s^2 - R_a^2}$. 6. Determine No-Load EMF $E_0$ from phasor analysis: $$\mathbf{E}_0 = \mathbf{V} + \mathbf{I}_a (R_a + jX_s) = (V\cos\phi + I_a R_a) + j(V\sin\phi \pm I_a X_s)$$ $$E_0 = \sqrt{(V\cos\phi + I_a R_a)^2 + (V\sin\phi \pm I_a X_s)^2}$$ where $(+)$ is used for lagging PF and $(-)$ for leading PF. Critique: The EMF method assumes magnetic linearity and uses unsaturated $Z_s$. Because saturation lowers the actual reactance, the EMF method yields an $E_0$ larger than the real value, predicting higher regulation. Hence, it is known as the Pessimistic Method.

Method B: MMF (Ampere-Turn / Rothert's) Method: The MMF method treats armature reaction as an MMF rather than a fictitious reactance drop: 1. Determine $F_{f1}$: Field current required to generate rated terminal voltage $V$ on OCC plus armature resistance drop. 2. Determine $F_{ar}$: Field current required to circulate rated armature current on SCC against armature reaction and leakage reactance. 3. Combine field MMF vectors at an angle of $(90^\circ \pm \phi)$: $$F_R = \sqrt{F_{f1}^2 + F_{ar}^2 - 2 F_{f1} F_{ar} \cos(90^\circ + \phi)} = \sqrt{F_{f1}^2 + F_{ar}^2 + 2 F_{f1} F_{ar} \sin\phi}$$ 4. From the OCC curve, read $E_0$ corresponding to resultant field current $F_R$. Critique: Because it understates saturation, the MMF method yields an $E_0$ smaller than the actual value. Hence, it is known as the Optimistic Method.

3. Blondel's Two-Reaction Theory for Salient Pole Alternators: In salient pole machines, the air-gap is non-uniform: it is minimal along the direct axis ($d$-axis, center of pole) and maximal along the quadrature axis ($q$-axis, interpolar space). Consequently, the reluctance along the $q$-axis is much higher than along the $d$-axis: $$\mathcal{R}_q \gg \mathcal{R}_d \implies X_d > X_q$$ where $X_d$ is direct-axis synchronous reactance and $X_q$ is quadrature-axis synchronous reactance.

Armature Current Resolution: Armature current $\mathbf{I}_a$ is resolved into two orthogonal components: - Direct-axis current $I_d = I_a \sin(\delta + \phi)$ (produces flux purely along $d$-axis). - Quadrature-axis current $I_q = I_a \cos(\delta + \phi)$ (produces flux purely along $q$-axis). where $\delta$ is the power (rotor) angle and $\phi$ is the load power factor angle.

Terminal Voltage Phasor Equation: $$\mathbf{E}_0 = \mathbf{V} + I_a R_a + j I_d X_d + j I_q X_q$$ Projecting along the $d$ and $q$ axes (neglecting $R_a$): $$V\sin\delta = I_q X_q \implies I_q = \frac{V\sin\delta}{X_q}$$ $$E_0 = V\cos\delta + I_d X_d \implies I_d = \frac{E_0 - V\cos\delta}{X_d}$$

Determination of Power Angle $\delta$: From the geometry of the phasor diagram: $$\tan\delta = \frac{I_a X_q \cos\phi \pm I_a R_a \sin\phi}{V + I_a X_q \sin\phi \mp I_a R_a \cos\phi}$$ Once $\delta$ is calculated, $I_d$ and $I_q$ are determined, and excitation EMF is: $$E_0 = V\cos\delta + I_d X_d + I_q R_a$$

Power-Angle Equation & Reluctance Torque: Total electrical power delivered per phase is: $$P = V I_a \cos\phi = V (I_q \cos\delta + I_d \sin\delta)$$ Substituting $I_d$ and $I_q$: $$P = V \left( \frac{V\sin\delta}{X_q} \cos\delta + \frac{E_0 - V\cos\delta}{X_d} \sin\delta \right) = \frac{E_0 V}{X_d}\sin\delta + V^2 \sin\delta\cos\delta \left( \frac{1}{X_q} - \frac{1}{X_d} \right)$$ Using $\sin\delta\cos\delta = \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\delta$: $$P = \frac{E_0 V}{X_d}\sin\delta + \frac{V^2}{2}\left( \frac{X_d - X_q}{X_d X_q} \right)\sin 2\delta$$ Physical Interpretation: - First Term $\frac{E_0 V}{X_d}\sin\delta$: Fundamental Electromagnetic Excitation Power, arising from interaction between rotor field excitation and stator field. - Second Term $\frac{V^2}{2}\left( \frac{X_d - X_q}{X_d X_q} \right)\sin 2\delta$: Reluctance Power, independent of field excitation $E_0$. It arises entirely from the saliency of the rotor ($X_d \neq X_q$), which causes the rotor to align with the minimum-reluctance position of the air-gap rotating field. Even if field excitation is completely lost ($E_0 = 0$), a salient-pole alternator can still deliver stable reluctance power up to $\delta = 45^\circ$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Synchronous machines are doubly excited AC machines operating strictly at synchronous speed $N_s = 120 f / P$. Their voltage regulation under changing load power factor is a critical stability parameter.

1. Generated EMF Equation & Winding Factors: For a 3-phase alternator with flux per pole $\Phi$ ($\text{Wb}$), frequency $f$ ($\text{Hz}$), and series turns per phase $T_{ph}$: $$E_{ph} = 4.44 k_p k_d f \Phi T_{ph} \quad \text{Volts}$$ - Pitch Factor (Chording Factor) $k_p$: Accounts for short-pitching coils by chording angle $\alpha$: $$k_p = \cos\left( \frac{\alpha}{2} \right)$$ Short pitching eliminates specific harmonic orders (e.g., setting $\alpha = 180^\circ / n$ eliminates the $n$-th harmonic) and reduces end-turn copper volume. - Distribution (Breadth) Factor $k_d$: Accounts for distributing coils over $m$ slots per pole per phase with slot pitch angle $\beta = 180^\circ / (\text{slots/pole})$: $$k_d = \frac{\sin(m\beta / 2)}{m \sin(\beta / 2)}$$

2. Voltage Regulation Determination: Voltage regulation is defined as: $$\%VR = \frac{|E_{0}| - |V|}{V} \times 100\%$$ where $E_0$ is the no-load open-circuit terminal voltage for the same excitation field current $I_f$ that yields rated terminal voltage $V$ at rated load.

Method A: Synchronous Impedance (EMF) Method: 1. Conduct Open-Circuit Test to obtain the Open-Circuit Characteristic (OCC: $V_{oc}$ vs $I_f$). 2. Conduct Short-Circuit Test to obtain the Short-Circuit Characteristic (SCC: $I_{sc}$ vs $I_f$). 3. For a specific field current $I_f$: $$Z_s = \left. \frac{V_{oc,ph}}{I_{sc,ph}} \right|_{I_f = \text{const}}$$ 4. Measure DC armature resistance and correct for skin effect: $R_a \approx 1.25 R_{dc}$. 5. Calculate Synchronous Reactance: $X_s = \sqrt{Z_s^2 - R_a^2}$. 6. Determine No-Load EMF $E_0$ from phasor analysis: $$\mathbf{E}_0 = \mathbf{V} + \mathbf{I}_a (R_a + jX_s) = (V\cos\phi + I_a R_a) + j(V\sin\phi \pm I_a X_s)$$ $$E_0 = \sqrt{(V\cos\phi + I_a R_a)^2 + (V\sin\phi \pm I_a X_s)^2}$$ where $(+)$ is used for lagging PF and $(-)$ for leading PF. Critique: The EMF method assumes magnetic linearity and uses unsaturated $Z_s$. Because saturation lowers the actual reactance, the EMF method yields an $E_0$ larger than the real value, predicting higher regulation. Hence, it is known as the Pessimistic Method.

Method B: MMF (Ampere-Turn / Rothert's) Method: The MMF method treats armature reaction as an MMF rather than a fictitious reactance drop: 1. Determine $F_{f1}$: Field current required to generate rated terminal voltage $V$ on OCC plus armature resistance drop. 2. Determine $F_{ar}$: Field current required to circulate rated armature current on SCC against armature reaction and leakage reactance. 3. Combine field MMF vectors at an angle of $(90^\circ \pm \phi)$: $$F_R = \sqrt{F_{f1}^2 + F_{ar}^2 - 2 F_{f1} F_{ar} \cos(90^\circ + \phi)} = \sqrt{F_{f1}^2 + F_{ar}^2 + 2 F_{f1} F_{ar} \sin\phi}$$ 4. From the OCC curve, read $E_0$ corresponding to resultant field current $F_R$. Critique: Because it understates saturation, the MMF method yields an $E_0$ smaller than the actual value. Hence, it is known as the Optimistic Method.

3. Blondel's Two-Reaction Theory for Salient Pole Alternators: In salient pole machines, the air-gap is non-uniform: it is minimal along the direct axis ($d$-axis, center of pole) and maximal along the quadrature axis ($q$-axis, interpolar space). Consequently, the reluctance along the $q$-axis is much higher than along the $d$-axis: $$\mathcal{R}_q \gg \mathcal{R}_d \implies X_d > X_q$$ where $X_d$ is direct-axis synchronous reactance and $X_q$ is quadrature-axis synchronous reactance.

Armature Current Resolution: Armature current $\mathbf{I}_a$ is resolved into two orthogonal components: - Direct-axis current $I_d = I_a \sin(\delta + \phi)$ (produces flux purely along $d$-axis). - Quadrature-axis current $I_q = I_a \cos(\delta + \phi)$ (produces flux purely along $q$-axis). where $\delta$ is the power (rotor) angle and $\phi$ is the load power factor angle.

Terminal Voltage Phasor Equation: $$\mathbf{E}_0 = \mathbf{V} + I_a R_a + j I_d X_d + j I_q X_q$$ Projecting along the $d$ and $q$ axes (neglecting $R_a$): $$V\sin\delta = I_q X_q \implies I_q = \frac{V\sin\delta}{X_q}$$ $$E_0 = V\cos\delta + I_d X_d \implies I_d = \frac{E_0 - V\cos\delta}{X_d}$$

Determination of Power Angle $\delta$: From the geometry of the phasor diagram: $$\tan\delta = \frac{I_a X_q \cos\phi \pm I_a R_a \sin\phi}{V + I_a X_q \sin\phi \mp I_a R_a \cos\phi}$$ Once $\delta$ is calculated, $I_d$ and $I_q$ are determined, and excitation EMF is: $$E_0 = V\cos\delta + I_d X_d + I_q R_a$$

Power-Angle Equation & Reluctance Torque: Total electrical power delivered per phase is: $$P = V I_a \cos\phi = V (I_q \cos\delta + I_d \sin\delta)$$ Substituting $I_d$ and $I_q$: $$P = V \left( \frac{V\sin\delta}{X_q} \cos\delta + \frac{E_0 - V\cos\delta}{X_d} \sin\delta \right) = \frac{E_0 V}{X_d}\sin\delta + V^2 \sin\delta\cos\delta \left( \frac{1}{X_q} - \frac{1}{X_d} \right)$$ Using $\sin\delta\cos\delta = \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\delta$: $$P = \frac{E_0 V}{X_d}\sin\delta + \frac{V^2}{2}\left( \frac{X_d - X_q}{X_d X_q} \right)\sin 2\delta$$ Physical Interpretation: - First Term $\frac{E_0 V}{X_d}\sin\delta$: Fundamental Electromagnetic Excitation Power, arising from interaction between rotor field excitation and stator field. - Second Term $\frac{V^2}{2}\left( \frac{X_d - X_q}{X_d X_q} \right)\sin 2\delta$: Reluctance Power, independent of field excitation $E_0$. It arises entirely from the saliency of the rotor ($X_d \neq X_q$), which causes the rotor to align with the minimum-reluctance position of the air-gap rotating field. Even if field excitation is completely lost ($E_0 = 0$), a salient-pole alternator can still deliver stable reluctance power up to $\delta = 45^\circ$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

$$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3Z_f} \ (\text{SLG}), \quad \frac{2H}{\omega_s}\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e, \quad \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}}(P_m - P_e)d\delta = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}}(P_e - P_m)d\delta$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Modern electrical power systems demand rigorous analytical modeling to ensure continuous stability under dynamic electromechanical disturbances and fault contingencies.

1. Transmission Line ABCD Parameters: Transmission lines are represented as two-port four-terminal networks relating sending-end voltage/current to receiving-end quantities: $$\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{V}_S \\ \mathbf{I}_S \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} A & B \\ C & D \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{V}_R \\ \mathbf{I}_R \end{bmatrix}$$ Properties of passive linear reciprocal transmission lines: - Reciprocity: $A D - B C = 1$ - Symmetry: $A = D$ (for identical bilateral lines)

Line Classifications: - Short Line ($L < 80\text{ km}$, shunt capacitance neglected): $$A = D = 1, \quad B = Z = R + j\omega L, \quad C = 0$$ - Medium Line ($80\text{ km} \le L \le 250\text{ km}$): - Nominal-$\pi$ Model (total admittance $Y$ split equally at ends $Y/2$): $$A = D = 1 + \frac{Y Z}{2}, \quad B = Z, \quad C = Y\left(1 + \frac{Y Z}{4}\right)$$ - Nominal-T Model (total impedance $Z$ split equally $Z/2$): $$A = D = 1 + \frac{Y Z}{2}, \quad B = Z\left(1 + \frac{Y Z}{4}\right), \quad C = Y$$

2. Per-Unit (pu) Representation: The per-unit value simplifies computational analysis across multiple voltage levels: $$\text{Value}_{pu} = \frac{\text{Actual Value}}{\text{Base Value}}$$ Base impedance: $Z_{base} = \frac{(V_{base,LL})^2}{S_{base,3\phi}} \ \Omega$. Base change formula: $$Z_{pu}^{new} = Z_{pu}^{old} \times \left( \frac{V_{base}^{old}}{V_{base}^{new}} \right)^2 \times \left( \frac{S_{base}^{new}}{S_{base}^{old}} \right)$$

3. Symmetrical and Unsymmetrical Fault Analysis: Symmetrical (Three-Phase) Fault: All three phases short simultaneously. The system remains balanced; per-phase analysis using Thévenin equivalent impedance $Z_{th} = Z_1$ suffices: $$I_f = \frac{V_f}{Z_1 + Z_f}, \quad \text{Short-Circuit MVA} = \sqrt{3} V_L I_f = \frac{\text{Base MVA}}{Z_{pu}}$$

Unsymmetrical Fault Analysis via Symmetrical Components: Fortescue's Theorem resolves any unbalanced set of 3-phase vectors into three symmetrical components: $$\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{I}_a \\ \mathbf{I}_b \\ \mathbf{I}_c \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{I}_{a0} \\ \mathbf{I}_{a1} \\ \mathbf{I}_{a2} \end{bmatrix}$$ where $a = 1 \angle 120^\circ = -0.5 + j0.866$, and $a^2 = 1 \angle 240^\circ$.

Sequence Network Connections for Shunt Faults: 1. Single Line-to-Ground (SLG) Fault (Phase 'a' to ground): - Boundary conditions: $I_b = 0, I_c = 0, V_a = I_a Z_f$. - Symmetrical components: $I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}I_a$. - Network Interconnection: Positive, Negative, and Zero sequence networks connected in series: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ $$I_f = I_a = 3 I_{a1} = \frac{3 E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ 2. Line-to-Line (LL) Fault (Between Phase 'b' and Phase 'c'): - Boundary conditions: $I_a = 0, I_b = -I_c, V_b - V_c = I_b Z_f$. - Sequence components: $I_{a0} = 0, I_{a2} = -I_{a1}$. - Network Interconnection: Positive and Negative sequence networks connected in parallel opposing: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f}$$ $$I_f = I_b = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1}$$ 3. Double Line-to-Ground (DLG) Fault (Phases 'b' and 'c' to ground): - Boundary conditions: $I_a = 0, V_b = V_c = (I_b + I_c)Z_f$. - Network Interconnection: Positive, Negative, and Zero sequence networks connected in parallel: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + \frac{Z_2 (Z_0 + 3 Z_f)}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}}$$

4. Rotor Dynamics & The Swing Equation: The electromechanical equation governing rotor angular motion of a synchronous generator is derived from Newton's second law: $$J \frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = T_m - T_e = T_a$$ where $J$ is moment of inertia ($\text{kg}\cdot\text{m}^2$), $\theta_m$ is mechanical rotor position, $T_m$ is shaft mechanical torque, $T_e$ is electromagnetic torque, and $T_a$ is accelerating torque. Expressing angular position relative to synchronously rotating reference frame: $\theta_m = \omega_{sm} t + \delta_m$. Differentiating twice: $\frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2}$. Multiplying both sides by synchronous speed $\omega_{sm}$: $$J \omega_{sm} \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$ Defining the Angular Momentum $M = J \omega_{sm}$ and Inertia Constant $H$: $$H = \frac{\text{Stored Kinetic Energy at Synchronous Speed}}{\text{Machine Rating } S_n} = \frac{\frac{1}{2} J \omega_{sm}^2}{S_n} \ \frac{\text{MJ}}{\text{MVA}}$$ $$M = \frac{2 H S_n}{\omega_s} \quad \implies \quad M \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$ In per-unit form: $$\frac{2 H}{\omega_s} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu}$$ where $\omega_s = 2\pi f$ ($\text{rad/s}$) and $P_e = P_{max} \sin\delta$. This non-linear second-order differential equation is the Swing Equation.

5. Transient Stability & The Equal Area Criterion: For a single machine connected to an infinite bus (SMIB), stability following a disturbance can be assessed without numerically solving the non-linear swing equation by applying the Equal Area Criterion. Multiplying the swing equation by $2 \frac{d\delta}{dt}$: $$2 \frac{d\delta}{dt} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{\omega_s}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt} \implies \frac{d}{dt}\left[ \left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2 \right] = \frac{2\omega_s}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt}$$ Integrating from initial angle $\delta_0$ to maximum swing angle $\delta_{max}$: $$\left( \frac{d\delta}{dt} \right)^2 = \frac{2\omega_s}{M} \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) d\delta$$ For the system to remain stable, the rotor speed deviation $\frac{d\delta}{dt}$ must return to zero at some maximum angle $\delta_{max}$, meaning: $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) d\delta = 0$$ Dividing into accelerating ($A_1$) and decelerating ($A_2$) energy areas: $$A_1 = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} (P_m - P_e^{fault}) d\delta = A_2 = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_e^{post} - P_m) d\delta$$ - Critical Clearing Angle ($\delta_{cr}$): The maximum allowable angle at which circuit breakers can isolate the fault such that $A_1 = A_2$. For a fault cleared on an unmodified system with $P_e^{fault} = 0$: $$P_m (\delta_{cr} - \delta_0) = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{max}\sin\delta - P_m) d\delta$$ $$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{P_m}{P_{max}}(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) + \cos\delta_{max}$$ where $\delta_{max} = \pi - \delta_0$. If the fault is cleared before $\delta$ reaches $\delta_{cr}$, the system successfully decelerates and remains transiently stable.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Modern electrical power systems demand rigorous analytical modeling to ensure continuous stability under dynamic electromechanical disturbances and fault contingencies.

1. Transmission Line ABCD Parameters: Transmission lines are represented as two-port four-terminal networks relating sending-end voltage/current to receiving-end quantities: $$\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{V}_S \\ \mathbf{I}_S \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} A & B \\ C & D \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{V}_R \\ \mathbf{I}_R \end{bmatrix}$$ Properties of passive linear reciprocal transmission lines: - Reciprocity: $A D - B C = 1$ - Symmetry: $A = D$ (for identical bilateral lines)

Line Classifications: - Short Line ($L < 80\text{ km}$, shunt capacitance neglected): $$A = D = 1, \quad B = Z = R + j\omega L, \quad C = 0$$ - Medium Line ($80\text{ km} \le L \le 250\text{ km}$): - Nominal-$\pi$ Model (total admittance $Y$ split equally at ends $Y/2$): $$A = D = 1 + \frac{Y Z}{2}, \quad B = Z, \quad C = Y\left(1 + \frac{Y Z}{4}\right)$$ - Nominal-T Model (total impedance $Z$ split equally $Z/2$): $$A = D = 1 + \frac{Y Z}{2}, \quad B = Z\left(1 + \frac{Y Z}{4}\right), \quad C = Y$$

2. Per-Unit (pu) Representation: The per-unit value simplifies computational analysis across multiple voltage levels: $$\text{Value}_{pu} = \frac{\text{Actual Value}}{\text{Base Value}}$$ Base impedance: $Z_{base} = \frac{(V_{base,LL})^2}{S_{base,3\phi}} \ \Omega$. Base change formula: $$Z_{pu}^{new} = Z_{pu}^{old} \times \left( \frac{V_{base}^{old}}{V_{base}^{new}} \right)^2 \times \left( \frac{S_{base}^{new}}{S_{base}^{old}} \right)$$

3. Symmetrical and Unsymmetrical Fault Analysis: Symmetrical (Three-Phase) Fault: All three phases short simultaneously. The system remains balanced; per-phase analysis using Thévenin equivalent impedance $Z_{th} = Z_1$ suffices: $$I_f = \frac{V_f}{Z_1 + Z_f}, \quad \text{Short-Circuit MVA} = \sqrt{3} V_L I_f = \frac{\text{Base MVA}}{Z_{pu}}$$

Unsymmetrical Fault Analysis via Symmetrical Components: Fortescue's Theorem resolves any unbalanced set of 3-phase vectors into three symmetrical components: $$\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{I}_a \\ \mathbf{I}_b \\ \mathbf{I}_c \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{I}_{a0} \\ \mathbf{I}_{a1} \\ \mathbf{I}_{a2} \end{bmatrix}$$ where $a = 1 \angle 120^\circ = -0.5 + j0.866$, and $a^2 = 1 \angle 240^\circ$.

Sequence Network Connections for Shunt Faults: 1. Single Line-to-Ground (SLG) Fault (Phase 'a' to ground): - Boundary conditions: $I_b = 0, I_c = 0, V_a = I_a Z_f$. - Symmetrical components: $I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}I_a$. - Network Interconnection: Positive, Negative, and Zero sequence networks connected in series: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ $$I_f = I_a = 3 I_{a1} = \frac{3 E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ 2. Line-to-Line (LL) Fault (Between Phase 'b' and Phase 'c'): - Boundary conditions: $I_a = 0, I_b = -I_c, V_b - V_c = I_b Z_f$. - Sequence components: $I_{a0} = 0, I_{a2} = -I_{a1}$. - Network Interconnection: Positive and Negative sequence networks connected in parallel opposing: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f}$$ $$I_f = I_b = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1}$$ 3. Double Line-to-Ground (DLG) Fault (Phases 'b' and 'c' to ground): - Boundary conditions: $I_a = 0, V_b = V_c = (I_b + I_c)Z_f$. - Network Interconnection: Positive, Negative, and Zero sequence networks connected in parallel: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + \frac{Z_2 (Z_0 + 3 Z_f)}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}}$$

4. Rotor Dynamics & The Swing Equation: The electromechanical equation governing rotor angular motion of a synchronous generator is derived from Newton's second law: $$J \frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = T_m - T_e = T_a$$ where $J$ is moment of inertia ($\text{kg}\cdot\text{m}^2$), $\theta_m$ is mechanical rotor position, $T_m$ is shaft mechanical torque, $T_e$ is electromagnetic torque, and $T_a$ is accelerating torque. Expressing angular position relative to synchronously rotating reference frame: $\theta_m = \omega_{sm} t + \delta_m$. Differentiating twice: $\frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2}$. Multiplying both sides by synchronous speed $\omega_{sm}$: $$J \omega_{sm} \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$ Defining the Angular Momentum $M = J \omega_{sm}$ and Inertia Constant $H$: $$H = \frac{\text{Stored Kinetic Energy at Synchronous Speed}}{\text{Machine Rating } S_n} = \frac{\frac{1}{2} J \omega_{sm}^2}{S_n} \ \frac{\text{MJ}}{\text{MVA}}$$ $$M = \frac{2 H S_n}{\omega_s} \quad \implies \quad M \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$ In per-unit form: $$\frac{2 H}{\omega_s} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu}$$ where $\omega_s = 2\pi f$ ($\text{rad/s}$) and $P_e = P_{max} \sin\delta$. This non-linear second-order differential equation is the Swing Equation.

5. Transient Stability & The Equal Area Criterion: For a single machine connected to an infinite bus (SMIB), stability following a disturbance can be assessed without numerically solving the non-linear swing equation by applying the Equal Area Criterion. Multiplying the swing equation by $2 \frac{d\delta}{dt}$: $$2 \frac{d\delta}{dt} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{\omega_s}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt} \implies \frac{d}{dt}\left[ \left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2 \right] = \frac{2\omega_s}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt}$$ Integrating from initial angle $\delta_0$ to maximum swing angle $\delta_{max}$: $$\left( \frac{d\delta}{dt} \right)^2 = \frac{2\omega_s}{M} \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) d\delta$$ For the system to remain stable, the rotor speed deviation $\frac{d\delta}{dt}$ must return to zero at some maximum angle $\delta_{max}$, meaning: $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) d\delta = 0$$ Dividing into accelerating ($A_1$) and decelerating ($A_2$) energy areas: $$A_1 = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} (P_m - P_e^{fault}) d\delta = A_2 = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_e^{post} - P_m) d\delta$$ - Critical Clearing Angle ($\delta_{cr}$): The maximum allowable angle at which circuit breakers can isolate the fault such that $A_1 = A_2$. For a fault cleared on an unmodified system with $P_e^{fault} = 0$: $$P_m (\delta_{cr} - \delta_0) = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{max}\sin\delta - P_m) d\delta$$ $$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{P_m}{P_{max}}(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) + \cos\delta_{max}$$ where $\delta_{max} = \pi - \delta_0$. If the fault is cleared before $\delta$ reaches $\delta_{cr}$, the system successfully decelerates and remains transiently stable.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Modern electrical power systems demand rigorous analytical modeling to ensure continuous stability under dynamic electromechanical disturbances and fault contingencies.

1. Transmission Line ABCD Parameters: Transmission lines are represented as two-port four-terminal networks relating sending-end voltage/current to receiving-end quantities: $$\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{V}_S \\ \mathbf{I}_S \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} A & B \\ C & D \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{V}_R \\ \mathbf{I}_R \end{bmatrix}$$ Properties of passive linear reciprocal transmission lines: - Reciprocity: $A D - B C = 1$ - Symmetry: $A = D$ (for identical bilateral lines)

Line Classifications: - Short Line ($L < 80\text{ km}$, shunt capacitance neglected): $$A = D = 1, \quad B = Z = R + j\omega L, \quad C = 0$$ - Medium Line ($80\text{ km} \le L \le 250\text{ km}$): - Nominal-$\pi$ Model (total admittance $Y$ split equally at ends $Y/2$): $$A = D = 1 + \frac{Y Z}{2}, \quad B = Z, \quad C = Y\left(1 + \frac{Y Z}{4}\right)$$ - Nominal-T Model (total impedance $Z$ split equally $Z/2$): $$A = D = 1 + \frac{Y Z}{2}, \quad B = Z\left(1 + \frac{Y Z}{4}\right), \quad C = Y$$

2. Per-Unit (pu) Representation: The per-unit value simplifies computational analysis across multiple voltage levels: $$\text{Value}_{pu} = \frac{\text{Actual Value}}{\text{Base Value}}$$ Base impedance: $Z_{base} = \frac{(V_{base,LL})^2}{S_{base,3\phi}} \ \Omega$. Base change formula: $$Z_{pu}^{new} = Z_{pu}^{old} \times \left( \frac{V_{base}^{old}}{V_{base}^{new}} \right)^2 \times \left( \frac{S_{base}^{new}}{S_{base}^{old}} \right)$$

3. Symmetrical and Unsymmetrical Fault Analysis: Symmetrical (Three-Phase) Fault: All three phases short simultaneously. The system remains balanced; per-phase analysis using Thévenin equivalent impedance $Z_{th} = Z_1$ suffices: $$I_f = \frac{V_f}{Z_1 + Z_f}, \quad \text{Short-Circuit MVA} = \sqrt{3} V_L I_f = \frac{\text{Base MVA}}{Z_{pu}}$$

Unsymmetrical Fault Analysis via Symmetrical Components: Fortescue's Theorem resolves any unbalanced set of 3-phase vectors into three symmetrical components: $$\begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{I}_a \\ \mathbf{I}_b \\ \mathbf{I}_c \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{I}_{a0} \\ \mathbf{I}_{a1} \\ \mathbf{I}_{a2} \end{bmatrix}$$ where $a = 1 \angle 120^\circ = -0.5 + j0.866$, and $a^2 = 1 \angle 240^\circ$.

Sequence Network Connections for Shunt Faults: 1. Single Line-to-Ground (SLG) Fault (Phase 'a' to ground): - Boundary conditions: $I_b = 0, I_c = 0, V_a = I_a Z_f$. - Symmetrical components: $I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}I_a$. - Network Interconnection: Positive, Negative, and Zero sequence networks connected in series: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ $$I_f = I_a = 3 I_{a1} = \frac{3 E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ 2. Line-to-Line (LL) Fault (Between Phase 'b' and Phase 'c'): - Boundary conditions: $I_a = 0, I_b = -I_c, V_b - V_c = I_b Z_f$. - Sequence components: $I_{a0} = 0, I_{a2} = -I_{a1}$. - Network Interconnection: Positive and Negative sequence networks connected in parallel opposing: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f}$$ $$I_f = I_b = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1}$$ 3. Double Line-to-Ground (DLG) Fault (Phases 'b' and 'c' to ground): - Boundary conditions: $I_a = 0, V_b = V_c = (I_b + I_c)Z_f$. - Network Interconnection: Positive, Negative, and Zero sequence networks connected in parallel: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + \frac{Z_2 (Z_0 + 3 Z_f)}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}}$$

4. Rotor Dynamics & The Swing Equation: The electromechanical equation governing rotor angular motion of a synchronous generator is derived from Newton's second law: $$J \frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = T_m - T_e = T_a$$ where $J$ is moment of inertia ($\text{kg}\cdot\text{m}^2$), $\theta_m$ is mechanical rotor position, $T_m$ is shaft mechanical torque, $T_e$ is electromagnetic torque, and $T_a$ is accelerating torque. Expressing angular position relative to synchronously rotating reference frame: $\theta_m = \omega_{sm} t + \delta_m$. Differentiating twice: $\frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2}$. Multiplying both sides by synchronous speed $\omega_{sm}$: $$J \omega_{sm} \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$ Defining the Angular Momentum $M = J \omega_{sm}$ and Inertia Constant $H$: $$H = \frac{\text{Stored Kinetic Energy at Synchronous Speed}}{\text{Machine Rating } S_n} = \frac{\frac{1}{2} J \omega_{sm}^2}{S_n} \ \frac{\text{MJ}}{\text{MVA}}$$ $$M = \frac{2 H S_n}{\omega_s} \quad \implies \quad M \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$ In per-unit form: $$\frac{2 H}{\omega_s} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu}$$ where $\omega_s = 2\pi f$ ($\text{rad/s}$) and $P_e = P_{max} \sin\delta$. This non-linear second-order differential equation is the Swing Equation.

5. Transient Stability & The Equal Area Criterion: For a single machine connected to an infinite bus (SMIB), stability following a disturbance can be assessed without numerically solving the non-linear swing equation by applying the Equal Area Criterion. Multiplying the swing equation by $2 \frac{d\delta}{dt}$: $$2 \frac{d\delta}{dt} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{\omega_s}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt} \implies \frac{d}{dt}\left[ \left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2 \right] = \frac{2\omega_s}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt}$$ Integrating from initial angle $\delta_0$ to maximum swing angle $\delta_{max}$: $$\left( \frac{d\delta}{dt} \right)^2 = \frac{2\omega_s}{M} \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) d\delta$$ For the system to remain stable, the rotor speed deviation $\frac{d\delta}{dt}$ must return to zero at some maximum angle $\delta_{max}$, meaning: $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) d\delta = 0$$ Dividing into accelerating ($A_1$) and decelerating ($A_2$) energy areas: $$A_1 = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} (P_m - P_e^{fault}) d\delta = A_2 = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_e^{post} - P_m) d\delta$$ - Critical Clearing Angle ($\delta_{cr}$): The maximum allowable angle at which circuit breakers can isolate the fault such that $A_1 = A_2$. For a fault cleared on an unmodified system with $P_e^{fault} = 0$: $$P_m (\delta_{cr} - \delta_0) = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{max}\sin\delta - P_m) d\delta$$ $$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{P_m}{P_{max}}(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) + \cos\delta_{max}$$ where $\delta_{max} = \pi - \delta_0$. If the fault is cleared before $\delta$ reaches $\delta_{cr}$, the system successfully decelerates and remains transiently stable.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$T(s) = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)} = \frac{\sum_{k=1}^N P_k \Delta_k}{\Delta}, \quad \Delta = 1 - \sum L_1 + \sum L_2 - \sum L_3 + \dots$$

Transfer Function & Mason\'s Rule: Block diagram algebra simplifies interconnected systems. Mason\'s gain formula evaluates closed-loop transfer functions directly by determining forward paths $P_k$, loops $L_i$, and non-touching loop determinants $\Delta, \Delta_k$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A control system regulates physical processes to achieve desired performance specifications. Transfer function modeling enables s-domain algebraic analysis of linear time-invariant (LTI) systems under zero initial conditions.

1. Concept of Transfer Function: The Transfer Function $G(s)$ of an LTI system is defined as the ratio of the Laplace transform of the output variable $C(s)$ to the Laplace transform of the input variable $R(s)$, assuming all initial conditions are identically zero: $$G(s) = \left. \frac{\mathcal{L}\{\text{output } c(t)\}}{\mathcal{L}\{\text{input } r(t)\}} \right|_{\text{all initial conditions} = 0} = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)}$$ The inverse Laplace transform of the transfer function yields the Impulse Response $g(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\{G(s)\}$, representing the system's time-domain response to a Dirac delta input $\delta(t)$.

Physical Modeling & Mechanical-Electrical Analogies: Using D'Alembert's principle, translational mechanical systems are governed by: $$M \frac{d^2 x(t)}{dt^2} + B \frac{dx(t)}{dt} + K x(t) = F(t)$$ - Force-Voltage ($F-V$) Analogy (Loop / Mesh Basis): $$L \frac{d^2 q}{dt^2} + R \frac{dq}{dt} + \frac{1}{C} q = v(t) \implies F \leftrightarrow v, \ v \leftrightarrow i, \ M \leftrightarrow L, \ B \leftrightarrow R, \ K \leftrightarrow \frac{1}{C}$$ - Force-Current ($F-I$) Analogy (Node Basis): $$C \frac{d^2 \phi}{dt^2} + \frac{1}{R} \frac{d\phi}{dt} + \frac{1}{L} \phi = i(t) \implies F \leftrightarrow i, \ v \leftrightarrow v, \ M \leftrightarrow C, \ B \leftrightarrow \frac{1}{R}, \ K \leftrightarrow \frac{1}{L}$$

2. Block Diagram Algebra & Reduction Rules: Complex multivariable and multiloop systems are graphically modeled by blocks representing transfer functions, connected by summing junctions and pickoff (branch) points. Key Reduction Equivalence Rules: 1. Cascaded (Series) Blocks: $$G_{eq}(s) = G_1(s) \cdot G_2(s)$$ 2. Parallel Blocks: $$G_{eq}(s) = G_1(s) \pm G_2(s)$$ 3. Elimination of Negative Feedback Loop: $$C(s) = G(s)[R(s) - H(s)C(s)] \implies \frac{C(s)}{R(s)} = \frac{G(s)}{1 + G(s)H(s)}$$ (For positive feedback, the denominator is $1 - G(s)H(s)$). 4. Moving a Summing Point Ahead of a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $1/G(s)$ in the feedback/diverted path. 5. Moving a Summing Point Behind a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $G(s)$ in the diverted path. 6. Moving a Pickoff Point Ahead of a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $G(s)$ in the branch path. 7. Moving a Pickoff Point Behind a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $1/G(s)$ in the branch path.

3. Signal Flow Graphs (SFG) and Mason's Gain Formula: A Signal Flow Graph is a directed network of nodes (representing system variables) connected by directed branches (representing functional transmittances). Definitions: - Forward Path: A continuous path tracing directed branches from input node to output node without passing through any node more than once. - Forward Path Gain ($P_k$): Product of branch gains along the $k$-th forward path. - Loop: A closed directed path that originates and terminates at the same node without traversing any other node more than once. - Loop Gain ($L_i$): Product of branch transmittances along the $i$-th closed loop. - Non-Touching Loops: Loops that do not share any common node.

Mason's Gain Formula: The overall closed-loop transfer function $T(s) = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)}$ is given by: $$T(s) = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)} = \frac{\sum_{k=1}^N P_k \Delta_k}{\Delta}$$ where: - $N$ = Total number of independent forward paths from input to output. - $P_k$ = Gain of the $k$-th forward path. - $\Delta$ = System Graph Determinant: $$\Delta = 1 - \sum_i L_i + \sum_{j,k} L_j L_k - \sum_{m,n,p} L_m L_n L_p + \dots$$ - $\sum L_i$ = Sum of all individual loop gains. - $\sum L_j L_k$ = Sum of gain products of all possible combinations of two non-touching loops. - $\sum L_m L_n L_p$ = Sum of gain products of all possible combinations of three non-touching loops. - $\Delta_k$ = Path Factor (Cofactor) of the $k$-th forward path: evaluated as the graph determinant of that part of the SFG which does not touch the $k$-th forward path (obtained by deleting all nodes belonging to forward path $P_k$).

Worked Example: Consider a multiloop system with two forward paths: - $P_1 = G_1 G_2 G_3$, $P_2 = G_4$ - Individual loops: $L_1 = -G_1 H_1$, $L_2 = -G_2 H_2$, $L_3 = -G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3$. - Suppose loops $L_1$ and $L_2$ share a node, but $L_1$ does not touch $L_4$ (if another isolated loop exists). If all loops touch each other, then $\sum L_j L_k = 0$. $$\Delta = 1 - (L_1 + L_2 + L_3) = 1 + G_1 H_1 + G_2 H_2 + G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3$$ Since forward path $P_1$ touches all loops, $\Delta_1 = 1$. If $P_2$ does not touch $L_2$, then $\Delta_2 = 1 - L_2 = 1 + G_2 H_2$. $$T(s) = \frac{P_1 \Delta_1 + P_2 \Delta_2}{\Delta} = \frac{G_1 G_2 G_3 (1) + G_4 (1 + G_2 H_2)}{1 + G_1 H_1 + G_2 H_2 + G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3}$$ Mason's formula computes the overall transfer function directly from inspection without tedious step-by-step block manipulations.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

A control system regulates physical processes to achieve desired performance specifications. Transfer function modeling enables s-domain algebraic analysis of linear time-invariant (LTI) systems under zero initial conditions.

1. Moving a Pickoff Point Ahead of a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $G(s)$ in the branch path. 7. Moving a Pickoff Point Behind a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $1/G(s)$ in the branch path.

Mason's Gain Formula: The overall closed-loop transfer function $T(s) = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)}$ is given by: $$T(s) = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)} = \frac{\sum_{k=1}^N P_k \Delta_k}{\Delta}$$ where: - $N$ = Total number of independent forward paths from input to output. - $P_k$ = Gain of the $k$-th forward path. - $\Delta$ = System Graph Determinant: $$\Delta = 1 - \sum_i L_i + \sum_{j,k} L_j L_k - \sum_{m,n,p} L_m L_n L_p + \dots$$ - $\sum L_i$ = Sum of all individual loop gains. - $\sum L_j L_k$ = Sum of gain products of all possible combinations of two non-touching loops. - $\sum L_m L_n L_p$ = Sum of gain products of all possible combinations of three non-touching loops. - $\Delta_k$ = Path Factor (Cofactor) of the $k$-th forward path: evaluated as the graph determinant of that part of the SFG which does not touch the $k$-th forward path (obtained by deleting all nodes belonging to forward path $P_k$).

Worked Example: Consider a multiloop system with two forward paths: - $P_1 = G_1 G_2 G_3$, $P_2 = G_4$ - Individual loops: $L_1 = -G_1 H_1$, $L_2 = -G_2 H_2$, $L_3 = -G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3$. - Suppose loops $L_1$ and $L_2$ share a node, but $L_1$ does not touch $L_4$ (if another isolated loop exists). If all loops touch each other, then $\sum L_j L_k = 0$. $$\Delta = 1 - (L_1 + L_2 + L_3) = 1 + G_1 H_1 + G_2 H_2 + G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3$$ Since forward path $P_1$ touches all loops, $\Delta_1 = 1$. If $P_2$ does not touch $L_2$, then $\Delta_2 = 1 - L_2 = 1 + G_2 H_2$. $$T(s) = \frac{P_1 \Delta_1 + P_2 \Delta_2}{\Delta} = \frac{G_1 G_2 G_3 (1) + G_4 (1 + G_2 H_2)}{1 + G_1 H_1 + G_2 H_2 + G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3}$$ Mason's formula computes the overall transfer function directly from inspection without tedious step-by-step block manipulations.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

A control system regulates physical processes to achieve desired performance specifications. Transfer function modeling enables s-domain algebraic analysis of linear time-invariant (LTI) systems under zero initial conditions.

1. Concept of Transfer Function: The Transfer Function $G(s)$ of an LTI system is defined as the ratio of the Laplace transform of the output variable $C(s)$ to the Laplace transform of the input variable $R(s)$, assuming all initial conditions are identically zero: $$G(s) = \left. \frac{\mathcal{L}\{\text{output } c(t)\}}{\mathcal{L}\{\text{input } r(t)\}} \right|_{\text{all initial conditions} = 0} = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)}$$ The inverse Laplace transform of the transfer function yields the Impulse Response $g(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\{G(s)\}$, representing the system's time-domain response to a Dirac delta input $\delta(t)$.

Physical Modeling & Mechanical-Electrical Analogies: Using D'Alembert's principle, translational mechanical systems are governed by: $$M \frac{d^2 x(t)}{dt^2} + B \frac{dx(t)}{dt} + K x(t) = F(t)$$ - Force-Voltage ($F-V$) Analogy (Loop / Mesh Basis): $$L \frac{d^2 q}{dt^2} + R \frac{dq}{dt} + \frac{1}{C} q = v(t) \implies F \leftrightarrow v, \ v \leftrightarrow i, \ M \leftrightarrow L, \ B \leftrightarrow R, \ K \leftrightarrow \frac{1}{C}$$ - Force-Current ($F-I$) Analogy (Node Basis): $$C \frac{d^2 \phi}{dt^2} + \frac{1}{R} \frac{d\phi}{dt} + \frac{1}{L} \phi = i(t) \implies F \leftrightarrow i, \ v \leftrightarrow v, \ M \leftrightarrow C, \ B \leftrightarrow \frac{1}{R}, \ K \leftrightarrow \frac{1}{L}$$

2. Block Diagram Algebra & Reduction Rules: Complex multivariable and multiloop systems are graphically modeled by blocks representing transfer functions, connected by summing junctions and pickoff (branch) points. Key Reduction Equivalence Rules: 1. Cascaded (Series) Blocks: $$G_{eq}(s) = G_1(s) \cdot G_2(s)$$ 2. Parallel Blocks: $$G_{eq}(s) = G_1(s) \pm G_2(s)$$ 3. Elimination of Negative Feedback Loop: $$C(s) = G(s)[R(s) - H(s)C(s)] \implies \frac{C(s)}{R(s)} = \frac{G(s)}{1 + G(s)H(s)}$$ (For positive feedback, the denominator is $1 - G(s)H(s)$). 4. Moving a Summing Point Ahead of a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $1/G(s)$ in the feedback/diverted path. 5. Moving a Summing Point Behind a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $G(s)$ in the diverted path. 6. Moving a Pickoff Point Ahead of a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $G(s)$ in the branch path. 7. Moving a Pickoff Point Behind a Block $G(s)$: Add a block $1/G(s)$ in the branch path.

3. Signal Flow Graphs (SFG) and Mason's Gain Formula: A Signal Flow Graph is a directed network of nodes (representing system variables) connected by directed branches (representing functional transmittances). Definitions: - Forward Path: A continuous path tracing directed branches from input node to output node without passing through any node more than once. - Forward Path Gain ($P_k$): Product of branch gains along the $k$-th forward path. - Loop: A closed directed path that originates and terminates at the same node without traversing any other node more than once. - Loop Gain ($L_i$): Product of branch transmittances along the $i$-th closed loop. - Non-Touching Loops: Loops that do not share any common node.

Mason's Gain Formula: The overall closed-loop transfer function $T(s) = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)}$ is given by: $$T(s) = \frac{C(s)}{R(s)} = \frac{\sum_{k=1}^N P_k \Delta_k}{\Delta}$$ where: - $N$ = Total number of independent forward paths from input to output. - $P_k$ = Gain of the $k$-th forward path. - $\Delta$ = System Graph Determinant: $$\Delta = 1 - \sum_i L_i + \sum_{j,k} L_j L_k - \sum_{m,n,p} L_m L_n L_p + \dots$$ - $\sum L_i$ = Sum of all individual loop gains. - $\sum L_j L_k$ = Sum of gain products of all possible combinations of two non-touching loops. - $\sum L_m L_n L_p$ = Sum of gain products of all possible combinations of three non-touching loops. - $\Delta_k$ = Path Factor (Cofactor) of the $k$-th forward path: evaluated as the graph determinant of that part of the SFG which does not touch the $k$-th forward path (obtained by deleting all nodes belonging to forward path $P_k$).

Worked Example: Consider a multiloop system with two forward paths: - $P_1 = G_1 G_2 G_3$, $P_2 = G_4$ - Individual loops: $L_1 = -G_1 H_1$, $L_2 = -G_2 H_2$, $L_3 = -G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3$. - Suppose loops $L_1$ and $L_2$ share a node, but $L_1$ does not touch $L_4$ (if another isolated loop exists). If all loops touch each other, then $\sum L_j L_k = 0$. $$\Delta = 1 - (L_1 + L_2 + L_3) = 1 + G_1 H_1 + G_2 H_2 + G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3$$ Since forward path $P_1$ touches all loops, $\Delta_1 = 1$. If $P_2$ does not touch $L_2$, then $\Delta_2 = 1 - L_2 = 1 + G_2 H_2$. $$T(s) = \frac{P_1 \Delta_1 + P_2 \Delta_2}{\Delta} = \frac{G_1 G_2 G_3 (1) + G_4 (1 + G_2 H_2)}{1 + G_1 H_1 + G_2 H_2 + G_1 G_2 G_3 H_3}$$ Mason's formula computes the overall transfer function directly from inspection without tedious step-by-step block manipulations.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 3.1: Block Diagram Transformation Equivalence Rules and Signal Flow Graph Construction — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
